For the port this ruling stands: the guard and the rule refuse only a spawned agent (src/gobby/releases/activation.py:21-32; src/gobby/install/shared/workflows/rules/worker-safety/no-daemon-management.yaml:5-6, 18).

**Resolved by:** ruling 11

### D31: How is the map applied to the 447 open #21542 nodes?

**Default:** At chunk-task creation on P (start gate item 10) every open node is dispositioned (python3 -I scripts/port/check_dispositions.py docs/port/open-nodes.json docs/port/map-v2.json docs/port/dispositions/*.json exits 0): a node owned by a chunk closes as superseded by that chunk's task, or done where its criterion is already met (#25582, #25577, and #22340 under D2), except #25486, which stays open as TMUX-PURGE's own task (D129); map-root containers close; and every old blocked_by edge among them is dropped, because the chunk DAG replaces them. Named consequences: #23275 to #24359 is deleted; #24711 moves to CP-FINAL-CUT and #24712 re-anchors on #24708; the nodes of the earlier map's end-of-port cutover move to the cutovers that now hold their work (#25475 to CP-RUNNER-CUT, #24882 and #24886 to CP-MCP-CUT), #24474 to CP-TEMPLATE-READS-CUT, #25521 to CP-TEMPLATE-CLOSE-CUT and #25467 to R-RETIRE; #25024's edge on #21563 is replaced by MEM-3's re-binding; #21566 3.1's edge on #25207 is cut; #24576 and #24863 close as superseded. Dropping edges reverses some approved ones (#25460's #23275 to #25468, #25460 D6's #24149 edges, ROADMAP uses-edges, #24657's N1 edge), which map approval confirms. Ownership moves the same way for #24703 and #24704 (to S4-CIX), #25557 (to MEM-3, which completes it) and #24707, #25559 and #25111 (to CP-WAVE3-CUT, whose activation commit carries their flips over dormant builds, D37). Closes at chunk-task creation run leaves first. Closing a node's last open child also tries the node with that child's close reason (close_eligible_parent_chain, src/gobby/storage/tasks/_close.py:170-220). The closure policy leaves the node open when it carries a hold label (needs-decision, needs-planning or clean-window) or epic-devteam, is claimed, escalated or on hold, or is a non-epic with its own validation criteria (closure-preserves-reserved-ancestors, src/gobby/install/shared/workflows/rules/task-closure/general.yaml:14-28), and on a superseded try unless the node already holds an approved disposition attestation with that reason and a reference (:54-75), so each node the cascade skips is closed explicitly after its children. A done try needs no implementation attestation (:30-43), so a done child that closes last takes an unreserved parent with it as done, whatever the parent's disposition; among one parent's children, done closes therefore run before superseded ones. In the 2026-11-04 snapshot the hold-labeled nodes with open children are #21546, #21560, #21562, #21563, #21564, #21566, #21567, #21570, #21573, #21586, #21588, #21592, #23276, #24149, #24151, #24657, #24841, #25070, #25467, #25468 and #25486. #23275 closes only after #24359 closes and #24360 moves out of its subtree (D6). A linked commit blocks a superseded close: closure-refuses-linked-commits-on-no-work-close (src/gobby/install/shared/workflows/rules/task-closure/general.yaml:77-84), which 9fa887db53 added on 2026-11-06, refuses any close other than done while a commit is linked, and unlink_commit refuses a commit that is on the integration branch (src/gobby/tasks/commit_unlink.py:20-46). So Josh ruled on 2026-11-12 that the rule is off for this pass (ruling 24): the session switches its installed row off before the pass's first superseded close of a node with a linked commit and on again when the pass ends, before 0.9.0's push, and the commits stay linked. Where a disposition's rationale still says linked commits make a node's close done, this sentence wins. Of the open nodes with linked commits, #22340 closes done under D2, and #23275, #24657, #24841, #25252, #25254, #25577 and #25582 have met their criteria and close done, with the approved implementation attestation that an explicit done close needs (src/gobby/install/shared/workflows/rules/task-closure/general.yaml:30-43). #20202, #21559, #21565, #21571, #21575, #21586, #23277, #24148, #24359, #24603, #24659, #25253 and #25255 to #25259 have not, and they close as superseded by their owning chunks' tasks, as any other owned node does. Three done closes fall outside that pass, as their owners' text says: #25577 in TMUX-PURGE's session, #25582 in R0's session and #22340 at COMMS-1's landing, with its SHA (D2). While #25582 and #22340 stay open, closure-requires-closed-children (src/gobby/install/shared/workflows/rules/task-closure/general.yaml:45-52) keeps their ancestors open too, so R0's session closes #25468 after #25582, R-RETIRE closes #25467 (its goal), and #21544 closes with whichever of #22340 and #25467 closes last: the cascade takes it on a done close, and that session closes it explicitly otherwise. Closing an epic also archives the plan record rooted at it, at any close reason, explicitly or through the cascade: the plan's file moves to .gobby/plans/completed/ in the project's main checkout and its coverage manifest is deleted, both left uncommitted (src/gobby/storage/tasks/_close.py:211-219; src/gobby/mcp_proxy/tools/tasks/_lifecycle_close_finalization.py:107-119; src/gobby/hooks/event_handlers/_plan.py:13-36; src/gobby/storage/plans.py:290-354). In the 2026-11-04 snapshot those epics are #20202, #21559, #21560, #21563, #21565, #21575, #21580, #21582, #21585, #21587, #23277, #24148 and #24657. Briefs and cutover sessions cite those plans at their live paths, so when these closes finish, before the first chunk claim, Josh runs git restore -- .gobby/plans and git clean -f -- .gobby/plans/completed from his own shell in P's main checkout, where nothing else is at work yet, because no-destructive-git-interactive refuses git clean -f in the unspawned session that ran them (src/gobby/install/shared/workflows/rules/worker-safety/no-destructive-git-interactive.yaml:17) and the task gate refuses it a whole-directory git restore without a claim (src/gobby/workflows/enforcement/blocking.py:697-753), and commits neither the moves nor the deletions. Chunk tasks are direct children of #21542, so it stays open through this pass as the port epic, and POST-CLEAN's close as done closes it. Four open nodes are no chunk's work (D2, D6), so this pass settles them first, because an open child keeps its parent open: closure-requires-closed-children refuses an explicit close of #21544, and the cascade swallows that refusal with no error (src/gobby/storage/tasks/_close.py:207-208), so POST-CLEAN's close would leave #21542 open. #22075, #19652 and #24360 move out of the #21542 tree into D6's post-1.0 backlog with update_task and an empty parent_task_id (src/gobby/mcp_proxy/tools/tasks/_crud.py:545-562). #22075 carries the protected deferred-by-user label (.gobby/project.json:5-9), and update_task refuses that label to a spawned or seated session whatever the escalation state (src/gobby/mcp_proxy/tools/tasks/_authorization.py:139-159), so this pass runs in an unspawned, operator-opened session. #25597 closes as wont_do, with an approved disposition attestation whose evidence.reason is wont_do and that cites D2 (src/gobby/install/shared/workflows/rules/task-closure/general.yaml:54-65). #25935, which Josh added on 2026-11-12, is the exception to this pass: it stays open as port work and closes under D130. A node whose disposition names a decision and that a chunk owns (#21576, #21583, #24173, #25201, #25269 and #25328) closes as superseded by that chunk's task, as any other chunk-owned node does, once Josh accepts that decision's default. An epic's archive move also leaves its plan's path dirty in the main checkout, and closure-preserves-attributed-work (src/gobby/install/shared/workflows/rules/task-closure/general.yaml:5-12) then refuses any later close whose linked commits touch that path: #24148 and #24657 touch hub-mode.md, rooted at #21575, and #25254 touches gdaemon-ai-providers-family.md, rooted at #21587. On that refusal the session restores that one plan file with git restore -- <its path>, which the task gate lets through because it names a .md under .gobby (src/gobby/workflows/enforcement/blocking.py:421-445, 747), and retries, and it never commits the move.

**Rationale:** Parent-chain readiness (_queries.py:408-443) turns the old edges into ready-queue deadlocks once nodes move, and claim_task checks no blockers, so the chunk DAG is the only ordering that holds: at chunk-task creation it becomes one blocks edge per depends_on entry among the chunk tasks under the port epic, and gport start refuses a chunk until every depends_on task has closed as done and, except for a review, its commit is an ancestor of 0.9.0 HEAD.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D32: Code-index P8 removes code_index.nightly_repair_timeout_seconds and leaves the native nightly repair with no per-project timeout; no approval is recorded.

**Default:** Port the timeout as-is in CIX-2 and keep the config key. #24713 loses that key's removal and keeps its code deletions in S3-DELETE, and CP-FINAL-CUT's activation commit removes its other key, code_index.maintenance_log_file (D65).

**Rationale:** Port-as-is is the default for behaviour, and removing a safety timeout is a product change that #21586's empty attestations never approved.

### D33: Bundled rule YAML has 76 lookaround or backreference matches in at least 10 files, and Rust's regex crate rejects them.

**Default:** P0-DECISIONS audits each pattern. RULE-1A rewrites each into a linear-time form that both Python re and Rust regex accept, with a rule-17 PATCH bump per file, proven by the decision corpus on both engines. The rewrites and their pattern-compile test form RULE-1A's prepared commit, which the first checkpoint cut whose review packet is written after RULE-1A closes lists by SHA and lands in its step 6, at the latest CP-WAVE3-CUT (D39); the live daemon loads each rewritten file at that cut's promotion restart, so every rewrite is proven on Python's engine before it lands. A pattern with no equivalent goes to Josh (for example an exact-pinned fancy-regex dependency for that pattern only). User-authored patterns with lookaround are refused at create and update with a clear error, recorded as a contract change. Each rewrite is a template edit that A17-TEMPLATES allows only because it preserves behavior on Python's engine; if Josh reads ruling 15 as no edits at all, A17-TEMPLATES' if_wrong applies. The audit and rewrite also cover the rule files this repository keeps outside the bundled tree (D77), which sync to the same rows on every restart (.gobby/workflows/rules/quiet-hours.yaml:23 has a lookbehind in the operator's block rule; src/gobby/runner_init/storage.py:82). The native engine compiles every stored row, so a row whose pattern the regex crate rejects is skipped and counted as a parse failure, which fails open for a block rule: CP-WAVE4-REVIEW's packet lists every enabled stored row with such a pattern, read only from the live hub, and CP-WAVE4-CUT proceeds only with that list empty or each entry ruled on by Josh.

**Rationale:** Rewrites keep one linear-time engine on the hot path and need no new dependency, and RULE-1A is the first Rust code that compiles every bundled pattern.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D34: CI on 0.9.0: ci.yml runs on pushes to main, 0.4.9 and 0.5.0 and on pull requests into main and 0.5.0 (.github/workflows/ci.yml:3-5, 9-10); rust-ci.yml, filtered by paths, runs on pushes to main and 0.5.0 and on pull requests into main (.github/workflows/rust-ci.yml:4-5, 21-22). None includes 0.9.0.

**Default:** Add 0.9.0 to the push triggers and the pull_request triggers of both workflows in the start gate, and reword AGENTS.md:232's coverage sentence to name 0.9.0. Make both workflows required: each landing session checks the CI run of the commit it pushed, or a spawned session has the port dispatcher push and read it (D116), a red run is found work fixed before the next landing, and a cutover activates only a commit whose CI is green. Keep local validation (cargo tests, the isolated harness, gport check-slice, and scripts/port/check_coverage.py until gport ledger's diff is 0, then gport ledger) as every chunk's gate. S3-DELETE switches CI to Rust-only checks. The workflow named Release Branch CI stays on and advisory until S3-DELETE, and a run that a later push cancelled is superseded, not red (D85).

**Rationale:** 0.9.0 is the live integration branch (ruling 15) and is pushed after every land, so CI on it catches what one machine's toolchain misses before a cutover makes it live. A third workflow, the one named Release Branch CI (.github/workflows/postgres-pgsearch-smoke.yml), already runs on every push to 0.9.0, because its branch filter [0-9]*.[0-9]* matches it (.github/workflows/postgres-pgsearch-smoke.yml:3-10), and it runs pre-push-test.sh, pytest included; S3-DELETE removes its Python steps.

**Resolved by:** ruling 15

### D35: Node MCP execution: #21566 P3/P4 (node-commands v6, the node MCP handler, placement, the 4.1 pair proof) is node work in a Stage 2 plan that overlaps Stage 4 #25328 and #25329, and #25329's criterion 2 (a hub-invoked call runs on the node) contradicts #21566 Decision 5 ('With no caller, it runs on the local machine').

**Rationale:** Decision 26 puts node work after Stage 3. Keeping it in MUX-2 would chain a Stage 2 chunk onto Stage 4 contract versions, and Decision 5 is the approved plan's rule.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D36: How is a route family packaged so that one family can be rolled back (Josh's gtask example)?

**Default:** Families are library crates on gcore inside one gdaemon binary; per-family processes are rejected for 1.0. A family's rollback is a generation rollback: promotion keeps previous binary generations, and gobby rollback restores one (D38). Josh's gtask example in each phase: (1) Before its checkpoint, gtasks is in 0.9.0, promoted by the first checkpoint cut after its landing, but its compiled default is Proxy, so Python serves tasks. (2) In its window, outcome 3 restores the pinned generation, the one installed when the cutover set its hold, whose gtasks default is Proxy, and outcome 2 fixes forward with the pin held. (3) After its window, a gtasks break is fixed by restoring the previous generation, which is the previous checkpoint's last promotion, because only checkpoint cuts promote (D39), or by a one-line deactivation commit that flips gtasks back to Proxy. That deactivation path is bounded: it exists for gtasks' MCP tools only until CP-WAVE5-CUT, whose MCP-FLIP stops routing any tool to Python, and for its HTTP routes only until CP-PREDELETE-CUT, after which no route has a Python backend. (4) From those points on, the rollback restores the previous generation while it is retained, and otherwise the fix goes forward. Across an expand step none of these restores needs a backup restore, because every retained generation from CP-RUNNER-CUT on accepts the hub's expand-only receipts (SCHEMA-WINDOW, D22); across a drop, at CP-FINAL-CUT or POST-CLEAN, the backup restore comes first.

**Rationale:** Josh: 'each route family would be its own rust binary or library, with gcore being the main library... We should have the previous version as a backup binary that we can roll back to and fix if needed.' The library option keeps hot paths in-process (hook ingress, the rule pass, MCP dispatch) and keeps single-owner state (terminal host, MCP children, comms connections) in one process. Per-family processes would put IPC on every hot call and need a distributed owner for single-owner state. Retained generations serve the backup-binary half of his example whole.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D37: How does landed port code stay inactive on the live 0.9.0 checkout until its checkpoint cutover?

**Default:** Dormancy through code defaults plus generation rollback. Every code default equals today's behavior; each checkpoint's activation commit flips only that checkpoint's defaults; rollback restores the pinned generation. Family constructors are inert, because RouteTable::new builds every family's router even while the family is proxied (crates/gdaemon/src/front_door/routes.rs:89-109). A family's jobs register only when the family is active, because ActiveServices runs its jobs unconditionally (crates/gdaemon/src/state_services.rs:27-93). Tests choose a backend through the harness (front_door.routes in a temporary home) or a constructor parameter, never through a new production key or environment variable. No runtime switch is added. The existing front_door.routes key is never used on the live daemon: a bad value makes gdaemon exit at start (crates/gcore/src/bootstrap/front_door.rs:183-190), the restart preflight does not provably catch it (src/gobby/cli/daemon_preflight.py:60-63), a stale override would pin a family against later activations, and a flip through it needs a full restart anyway, while a generation restore needs no build. The runner's mode is gdaemon's compiled default, passed to the Python child through BackendConfig.environment (crates/gdaemon/src/lifecycle/backend.rs:58), with no key or flag. Another session's cutover cannot promote an activation commit early: each cutover sets GEN-ROLLBACK's hold and its owner-bound landing freeze at step 3, before its backup, so every promotion without the hold's token is refused and only the cutover's own session can land until the freeze clears. Every chunk proves its own dormancy: the isolated harness on code defaults replays every existing corpus unchanged.

**Rationale:** Josh confirmed that dormancy works through code defaults plus generation rollback, with no runtime switches. A runtime switch was considered only where docs/port/facts-F2.md shows one exists and is clearly cheaper: front_door.routes is the only one, and it fails both tests for the reasons above. Ruling 16 rules one out for the runner in any case ('The runner gets no dual mode and no runtime runner switch'). This is ruling 9 applied: transitional machinery stays only where it is the cheapest way to keep correctness evidence at each chunk boundary. Code defaults plus generation rollback add no key, and front_door.routes fails that test and the cheaper-than-a-generation-restore test for the reasons above. The worker marker and GOBBY_TERMINAL_ROUTE are child-contract values set only by gdaemon's compiled defaults, not switches (D19).

**Resolved by:** ruling 16 and Josh's confirmation (2026-11-03, about 19:25 CT)

### D38: How does a checkpoint cutover roll back, given that promotion replaces binaries in place today (src/gobby/install/bin_set_coherence.py:88-153)?

**Default:** GEN-ROLLBACK, the first 0.9.0 chunk after TMUX-PURGE (D129), adds generation-retaining promotion (three generations kept as copies with a manifest that records the source commit), a window hold and burned versions in check_binary_release (src/gobby/install/bin_release_identity.py:234), the --hold-token option on gobby cutover and gobby install, gmcp and gterm promotion in gobby cutover when their versions changed, a landing freeze bound to the hold's task, the embedded-input release fingerprint, the protected-run admission on hub-backup's stop, and a new gobby rollback verb: list; pin, pin extend and unpin; hold set, hold extend, hold status and hold clear; burn; and restore with a --bin member list. Restore defaults to gdaemon alone, takes the stamped set (gcode, gdaemon and ghook) after a cut that changed the schema identity, never burns the restored target's own version, and runs through promote_workspace_binary_set with every guard except three legitimate bypasses: no build, no catalog freshness test and no unchanged-sources check. It lands before any promotion but TMUX-PURGE's, so the generation that the purge left installed is retained, and before CP-RUNNER-CUT. Its live drill promotes and restores gdaemon between checkpoints, a planned exception to ruling 20's promotion only at checkpoint cuts, so Josh's answer on D38 covers the drill too; if he refuses it, CP-RUNNER-CUT's promotion is the first live use of retention and its rehearsal is the proof. V1A ports it to Rust and reads its on-disk format unchanged. From CP-RUNNER-CUT on, a restore across an expand step needs no backup restore: the retained gdaemon's schema plan accepts expand-only receipts above its head (SCHEMA-WINDOW), and the restore preflight compares the retained gdaemon with its own stamp, never with the checkout pin (D22). Because only checkpoint cuts promote (D39), each cut's restore target is the previous cut's last promotion. V1A ports GEN-ROLLBACK whole, and S3-DELETE deletes its Python modules.

**Rationale:** Ruling 16 needs a pinned pre-cutover generation to roll back to, and Josh's gtask example asks for 'the previous version as a backup binary'. Nothing retains a previous generation today, so every checkpoint's rollback depends on this chunk. Building it in Python first is the least mechanism: it extends the promotion path every cutover already uses, and V1A ports that whole path later.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D39: Promotion cadence on the live 0.9.0 checkout. Ruling 14's notes say: 'Settled item 15 and the R7 row are revised: promotion happens at checkpoint cutovers.'

**Default:** Option B, amended. Every landing that changes gdaemon bumps its patch version, taking the next free version at landing. gcode and ghook, like gmcp, gclient and gterm, are bumped only in a cut's shipping commits (its activation commit, each outcome-2 fix commit and its deactivation commit) and in emergency cutover commits, because their pins are runtime floors (src/gobby/code_index/gcode_versions.py:5-6; src/gobby/hooks/runtime_compat.py:17, 28), so a landing that changes gobby-core leaves both versions alone. Each cut's activation commit bumps every binary whose fingerprint changed since its last bump and is the shipping commit for gcode and ghook. Promotion happens only inside checkpoint cuts. Option B's per-landing exceptions fold into the cuts: schema inputs and any template landing after TPL are prepared commits that the next checkpoint cut that needs them lands in its step 6, after its backup and its rehearsal and immediately before its activation commit (D22), and a fix task's commit that carries either, or a gcode or ghook version, lands the same way (D40 item 3). During a cut's freeze, the cutover session lands those approved commits as the hold's owner (the owner-bound landing freeze set in step (3)). Between checkpoints nobody promotes on P, and nobody runs gobby install there, since it promotes gdaemon whenever the pin is above the installed version (src/gobby/cli/install_setup_gdaemon.py:141-170), except an emergency cutover on Josh's word; GEN-ROLLBACK's landing drill, run from an unspawned, operator-opened session after one verified hub-backup (ruling 17), is a planned exception, whose promotions and restore run between checkpoints; ruling 20 does not name it, so Josh approves it with D38. TMUX-PURGE's leaves, which promote before GEN-ROLLBACK lands, are the other (D129). A gobby fix that Game Goblins needs is made on P (D40 item 3). Chunk sessions may be spawned agents: a chunk session lands and bumps, and never promotes. Cutover sessions stay unspawned and operator-opened, because activation refuses spawned agents (src/gobby/releases/activation.py:21-32). Dormancy by default stays, because a cut promotes all of 0.9.0 HEAD. S3-DELETE lands unpromoted too: CP-FINAL-CUT's activation is its first promotion, and it is not a template landing in this sense, because no template content changes.

**Rationale:** Josh: 'promote binaries on checkpoints right? we don't want to run a full rust recompile every time a binary is updated', which matches ruling 14's notes ('promotion happens at checkpoint cutovers'). Promoting only in cuts puts every promotion under a rehearsal, a window and a recipe and frees chunk sessions from the unspawned rule, at the cost of larger promotions at each checkpoint, which the dormancy proof (D37) and each cut's rehearsal bound. Bumping gdaemon at landing keeps the release guard's changed-source check meaningful at the next promotion (src/gobby/install/bin_release_identity.py:234-273), and holding the gcode and ghook bumps for activation commits keeps their runtime floors at or below the installed binaries between cuts, so the code-index gateway and ghook keep working (src/gobby/code_index/gcode_gateway.py:831-832). The release guard refuses a stamped binary whose source changed under its installed version (src/gobby/install/bin_release_identity.py:234-309), and gcode and ghook take gobby-core by path (crates/gcode/Cargo.toml:40; crates/ghook/Cargo.toml:29), so every commit a cut promotes is a shipping commit.

**Resolved by:** Josh, 2026-11-03 ~23:57 CT, ruling 20

### D40: Port topology and branch flow.

**Default:** (1) Two machines run two independent gobby installs (ruling 24). P runs the port on 0.9.0: it is this laptop, and later Josh's Linux machine, which is then seeded the way A is (D128). A is the Game Goblins machine: a restored copy of P's hub with its main checkout on origin's 0.5.0 at the cut SHA, where nobody edits gobby. 0.5.0 freezes at the cut (ruling 15), and no branch forks from 0.9.0. (2) A is seeded by restoring P's verified hub backup, taken after 0.9.0's push, once the chunk tasks exist (start gate items 17, 18 and 21). The restore keeps every row as it is, seq_num and path_cache included. Never seed a machine through the tasks.jsonl import (src/gobby/sync/tasks.py): it numbers any row that arrives without a seq_num from MAX(seq_num)+1 (:172). A gets P's secret KEK and its own machine id, has the gobby project's seat-launching cron jobs and its Telegram channel disabled before its first daemon start, and rebuilds the stores the backup does not carry; item 17 has the steps, and none of them gates the port. (3) Nothing flows from A to P. While ruling 24 stands the pending list is empty by definition: each review's packet says so in one line, and its cutover picks nothing and writes no picks.md commit. The pick steps stay in every review and cutover recipe, unused. If Josh later lets another install edit gobby, that ruling names its branch, and this item defines the pending list again before the next review; the definition of 2026-11-05 is this decision's text at 08f709e822. A gobby fix that Game Goblins needs is made on P as its own task, a child of #21542, so the port dispatcher starts it (D116): it follows the chunk protocol, lands and never promotes. If it carries a schema input, a gcode or ghook version or a post-TPL template, it is a prepared commit, which the next cut that lands schema inputs lands in its step 6 (D22); CP-RUNNER-CUT lands none. It reaches A by adoption (item 4). A fix to Python that an open chunk has already ported reaches that chunk as a Found Work line in docs/port/record.md, which the fix task's landing adds, and that chunk reads it at protocol step 2 or after its next merge; once the owning chunk is closed, the fix task ports the change itself. A fix that changes an answer a corpus recorded carries the re-recorded cases, as a fix under D18 does. (4) A cutover that closes as done tags the last commit it promoted on 0.9.0 as port/<checkpoint id>, an annotated tag whose message lists the SHAs of the prepared, regeneration and activation commits it landed, and pushes the tag: the commit that step 6 promoted after outcome 1, or the last fix-forward commit after outcome 2's final clean window. A cut held open by outcome 3 is tagged only when its retry closes. Every cutover recipe replays on any install: its own verified backup, the same schema inputs, promotion and activation, and no recipe names state that exists only on P. A adopts checkpoints when Josh decides, in checkpoint order, port/CP-RUNNER-CUT first, never skipping a tag; to catch up, it adopts each tag it has not yet adopted in turn, each with its own window. An adoption commits nothing: A never commits, merges or pushes gobby. It runs that cutover's nine steps on A's own install. Steps 1 to 5 run with the main checkout where it is: A's own announcement and live reads; a hold, a pin and a landing freeze bound to A's own adoption task; A's verified backup; and the rehearsal, which runs from a detached worktree at the tag. In step 6's place A moves its main checkout to the tag (the first adoption creates a local branch 0.9.0 there, and later ones fast-forward it) and promotes; steps 7 to 9 follow. A never has a tag's tree in its main checkout before its own backup and rehearsal: for a cut with schema inputs, the tag's pin would make hub-backup's start-half proof and every restart refuse (D22), and for every cut, the backup's own restart would start A on the tag's templates and activation defaults before any rehearsal. If an adoption rolls back, A runs the recipe's restores and then moves its main checkout back to the commit it ran before, which is the cut SHA before the first adoption, in place of a deactivation commit. A's first adoption is also its first install of GEN-ROLLBACK and of TMUX-PURGE's migrations, which delete rows (D129). Before its step 6, A has no gobby rollback, no hold and no pin, so its steps 1 to 5 are the announcement, the reads, the backup and the rehearsal, whose worktree at the tag supplies gobby rollback; and whatever the recipe's restore members are, a rollback of the first adoption also restores A's own step-4 backup. A restore burns the versions it replaces (GEN-ROLLBACK (b) and (c)), so after a rolled-back adoption A cannot promote that tag's versions again: Josh then waits for a later tag, or asks for a version-bump commit that P lands and tags (open problem). Each adoption is its own task on A: its session writes the step list from that cutover's recipe and this item, and Josh approves the list before step 1. (5) No chunk depends on cross-machine messaging, and nothing is sent from P to a session on A.

**Rationale:** Josh, 2026-11-12 (ruling 24): 'for now the plan is port work on this machine, and 0.5.0 with game goblins'; 'the game goblins copy will not be making edits to gobby'; 'I really just want to set up the port and get it done without it interfering with game goblins' daily responsibilities, and I really need to get this on a separate machine so i can just let the porting agents work and not get distracted by sidequests.' This replaces the topology of 2026-11-03, in which this machine forked 0.9.0-gg and its fixes reached 0.9.0 as reviewed cherry-picks. With no second line of gobby commits there is nothing to pick, to merge or to keep disjoint: no fork, no policy commit on A, and no numbering offset unless Josh asks for it (start gate item 18). The pick steps stay in the recipes because they are conditional on a list that is now empty, and taking them out of 26 reviewed recipes would change more reviewed text than leaving them. A adopts by moving its checkout to the tag because it has no commits of its own to merge, which also ends the adoption merge's conflict and version rules. Cutover step 3 still rebases onto the frozen tip before it lands, because land_commit escalates a candidate that shares a changed path with a moved tip (src/gobby/tasks/land_commit.py:703-727), escalation releases the task's claim (src/gobby/storage/tasks/_transitions.py:417-456), and a candidate that contains the tip fast-forwards (src/gobby/tasks/land_commit.py:737-787).

**Resolved by:** Josh, 2026-11-03 and 2026-11-04, rulings 18 and 21

### D41: Who evaluates the closure policy for native closes, ancestor auto-closes and landings before RULE-1B's engine serves it (tasks-family C1, #24151)?

**Default:** B0-CLOSE implements an interim adapter for gtasks' ClosurePolicy port (tasks-family 3.7 and 4.1) over one allow-listed worker op in R0's op table. The native snapshot builder reads the caller's agent definition and the effective rule rows under its own SHARE lock, as src/gobby/tasks/closure_snapshot.py:175 and :238 do, and sends them with the ClosureSnapshot; a small shim in R0's worker package rebuilds the ClosureSnapshot, the AgentDefinitionBody and the RuleDefinitionRow values (RuleDefinitionRow.from_row, src/gobby/storage/definitions/rules.py:88-106) and runs evaluate_closure_policy (src/gobby/tasks/closure_policy.py:40-91), so the op runs no query and evaluates the rows the closing transaction holds. A failed or timed-out call refuses the close or landing, as closure_snapshot_unavailable does (src/gobby/mcp_proxy/tools/tasks/_lifecycle_close.py:250-262), and in the vacated-parent walk it leaves the ancestor open, as a blocked decision does (src/gobby/storage/tasks/_close.py:207-208); it never allows a close. B0-CLOSE lands the op dormant and wires the walk that a reparent or delete runs (src/gobby/storage/tasks/_update_facade.py:120-134; src/gobby/storage/tasks/_lifecycle.py:543-554) into B0-WRITE's paths. CP-TEMPLATE-WRITES-CUT's activation commit binds the adapter, because update_task, delete_task and PATCH and DELETE /api/tasks/{task_id} go native there and their walk evaluates the policy; CP-TEMPLATE-CLOSE-CUT's activation commit then takes close_task, POST /api/tasks/{task_id}/close and land_commit native over it. RULE-1B implements the port natively (#24151's gworkflows adapter) and proves decision parity offline against REC-PILOT's closure vectors (#25501). HOOK-3 re-binds the port to it, and CP-WAVE4-CUT's activation commit flips that with the hook and tool-call rule passes (D27); the Rust side then no longer calls the op, and S3-DELETE deletes it. #24151's close-route activation under tasks-family C1 becomes that re-binding.

**Rationale:** C1 (operator-approved 2026-11-02) keeps the native close route proxied until #24151 supplies the evaluator, but RULE-1B depends on B0-CLOSE and SES-3A and serves only from HOOK-3. The close route is not the only consumer: Python's reparent and delete close a parent left with no open child through the same evaluator (src/gobby/storage/tasks/_close.py:194-206). With no interim evaluator, CP-TEMPLATE-WRITES-CUT and CP-TEMPLATE-CLOSE-CUT could activate only the writes and tools that evaluate no policy, and every chunk session's update_task, delete_task, close_task and land_commit would stay on Python until wave 4. The op does not fit D4's worker-op clause as written: that clause covers one-line registrations for single-owner services, while evaluate_closure_policy is pure code (src/gobby/tasks/closure_policy.py:1) that takes a ClosureSnapshot, an AgentDefinitionBody and a list of RuleDefinitionRow values (:40-42). The op therefore needs a shim that rebuilds all three from what Rust sends, and its only purpose is to move C1's activation earlier, so D4 lists it by name. It holds no live state and takes no lock of its own: the closing transaction holds its locks across the call, as it does across Python's in-process evaluation. An op that ran prepare_closure itself would instead wait on the task row lock that the native transaction already holds (src/gobby/tasks/closure_snapshot.py:156-159) while that transaction waits on the op. One rule engine stays the decision owner until HOOK-3, as GW-2's preflight op does for tool calls (D26, D27). Fallback, with no Python edit: close_task, POST /api/tasks/{task_id}/close and land_commit, and also update_task, delete_task and PATCH and DELETE /api/tasks/{task_id}, stay on Python until CP-WAVE4-CUT, which binds RULE-1B's policy through HOOK-3 and activates them; CP-TEMPLATE-WRITES-CUT activates only B0-WRITE's other writes, and CP-TEMPLATE-CLOSE-CUT activates only record_attestation, list_attestations, link_commit, unlink_commit, auto_link_commits, repair_closed_candidate and set_landing_freeze.

**Resolved by:** Josh, 2026-11-04 ~14:07 CT, ruling 22

### D47: How do the Projects plan's cross-runtime guards treat Python while it still runs: the project write fence's Python half (#25059) and the 26-file project.json guard (#25057)?

**Default:** (1) The fence's Python half is built, the one D4 exception this decision adds: #25059's edits to src/gobby/projects/write_fence.py, src/gobby/storage/hub/postgres.py and src/gobby/runner_init/services.py, with their tests, form their own prepared commit on port/prepared/PROJ-2, which CP-WAVE1-CUT lands in its step 6 after PROJ-2's schema commit, so Python's existing ProjectWriteFence API takes gcore's PostgreSQL advisory locks (crates/gcore/src/project_fence.rs) from the restart that activates native hard purge. Every native derived writer takes the same gcore fence (Projects plan 5.1), the memory family's writers among them (.gobby/plans/memory-family.md, write coordination). (2) #25057's 26 Python targets (Projects plan 4.5a) are not built, apart from the policy writers' bug fix under D4's bug-fix clause, which REC-PLATFORM lands (D64). PROJ-2 builds 4.5a's native lock protocol only (crates/gprojects/src/projects/metadata_lock.rs): the owner-only sidecar .gobby/.project.json.lock under the same exclusive flock that Python's project initialization already takes (src/gobby/utils/durable_file.py:26-38; src/gobby/utils/project_init.py:153, 192), taken before the project row lock. HTTP PUT and PATCH /api/projects/{project_id} go Native with the family at CP-WAVE1-CUT in Python's update order: under that flock and the project row lock, one validated candidate published by atomic replace, then the SQL commit, so Python's lock-free readers and writers see what Python's own update shows them today (D19). 4.6's recovery journal and commit witness (.gobby/.project-update-recovery/ and the project_file_mutations table, whose migration lands unused in PROJ-2's schema commit) sit behind a compiled default that CP-FINAL-CUT's activation commit turns on, the first activation with no Python reader or writer of project.json left (S3-DELETE); that is 4.5a.7's gate, and D63 makes every native policy reader outside gprojects read through one guard before it. 4.6.7 is not built, and 4.6.8 covers native writers and the Python writers that take the sidecar lock: project initialization and D64's fixed policy writers. Alternative for (2): build #25057's 26 Python files in the fence's prepared commit, so that CP-WAVE1-CUT turns the journal on with the routes, as the Projects plan specifies. S3-DELETE deletes the fence's Python half with the rest of src/gobby.

**Rationale:** No activation order avoids a mixed fence window: native hard purge activates with the family at CP-WAVE1-CUT and the native memory writers at CP-WAVE3-CUT, while Python's fenced writers run until CP-WAVE5-CUT, the MCP proxy's semantic tool index last (src/gobby/mcp_proxy/semantic_search.py:244; the knowledge graph, src/gobby/memory/services/knowledge_graph/service.py:157-160), and Python's fence is in-process only (src/gobby/projects/write_fence.py, whose exclusive side is Python's purge, src/gobby/projects/purge.py:247). Without its Python half, hard purge would have to be refused in both runtimes from CP-WAVE1-CUT to CP-WAVE5-CUT. D19's same-lock-keys rule needs a lock that both runtimes take, and three Python files are the least mechanism that gives one. The metadata guard is different: it protects a stricter invariant than Python keeps today, because Python's update publishes project.json before its SQL commit and never restores it (Projects plan 4.6), and Python's approval and validation readers take no lock (src/gobby/servers/tool_approvals.py:271-292; src/gobby/config/validation_detection.py:280-293), as its savers took none until D64's bug fix. D19 imposes a stricter invariant only where Python already satisfies it, so the journal waits for the first activation with no Python left, and the update in Python's order keeps every native row and file equal to Python's until then. Building the guard would edit 26 Python files across chat, hooks, tasks, agents, workflows and the CLI, all deleted at S3-DELETE, to bring forward a hardening that no Python consumer needs before then. Keeping PUT and PATCH on Proxy instead is not available: S3-FREEZE allows no proxied row, and Python CLI writers run until CP-PREDELETE-CUT.

**Resolved by:** Josh, 2026-11-04 ~14:07 CT, ruling 22

### D48: What serves GET and PUT /api/files/user-md after #25051's namespace cutover, while callers that name the old path still run?

**Default:** The Projects family keeps claiming GET and PUT /api/files/user-md, Native since CP-WAVE1-CUT, with the same handler as the canonical /api/hub/user that CP-WAVE3-CUT's namespace cutover adds, behind a compiled default that POST-CLEAN's activation commit turns off (D37), after CP-NODE-CUT has updated the live node and a literal search finds no caller of the old path left. PROJ-2's isolated harness runs #25051's 3.5.2 and 3.5.4 with the namespace cutover's default on and that default off, over a fixture copy of the six waiver entries and their case citations. The real entries land only with the commits that turn their old paths off, the four attachment entries in CP-WAVE3-CUT's activation commit and the two USER.md entries in POST-CLEAN's, because a cited corpus case fails while its old path still answers 200. No Python file changes. The attachment paths cut over as #25051 specifies: /api/chat/attachments answers the ordinary 404 from CP-WAVE3-CUT on, when CHAT-1 replaces Python's attachment consumers and PROJ-2's consumer commit moves the web UI, the two skill intro references and the docs to /api/hub/chat/attachments and /api/hub/user. Alternative: follow Projects plan 3.5.2 as written, so the old USER.md paths answer 404 from CP-WAVE3-CUT on, and the live node's session starts lose USER.md and its remote install preflight fails until CP-NODE-CUT updates the node. The same rule holds for every citation: a cited case passes only on its native answer (tests/contracts/http_corpus.py:333-369; crates/gdaemon/tests/http_contracts.rs:411-510), so a citation lands in the activation commit that flips its surface, each cut's step 6 says so, and until then the owning chunk replays against a fixture copy (chunk protocol). Of the Projects plan's other behaviour entries, twelve land in CP-WAVE1-CUT's activation commit, which flips their routes, and projects.files-migrate.final-layout in CP-PREDELETE-CUT's commit A, where V5B's verb takes gobby files migrate. D84 states which differences take an entry and which take a known-bad row, and that no chunk landing commits an entry.

**Rationale:** Projects plan 3.5 makes both old paths answer 404 at the cutover (3.5.2) with no compatibility alias, but the live node's install still names the old USER.md path after CP-WAVE3-CUT: its session-start USER.md read and its USER.md writes in remote files mode (src/gobby/hooks/event_handlers/_session_start/profile.py:30-63, through src/gobby/files_home_http.py:9) and its remote install preflight probe (src/gobby/cli/installers/remote_preflight.py:28, 369-425) run until CP-NODE-CUT updates the node. On the hub both use the local files_home and call no route (profile.py:66-88). A 404 would drop USER.md from every session start on the node, whose seed logs the failure and stores empty content (profile.py:97-109), fail every USER.md write from the node, and fail every remote preflight. Pointing those Python constants at /api/hub/user is no alternative: the live node's install changes only at CP-NODE-CUT, and D4 rules out a Python client of a Rust endpoint. Keeping the old claim adds no handler, only a second path on the existing one, behind a compiled default like every other activation (D37), and POST-CLEAN turns it off once nothing names it.

**Resolved by:** Josh, 2026-11-04 ~14:07 CT, ruling 22

### D50: #25105 (AI plan 4.2) builds the full capability snapshot through #25072's voice_stt_available(voice_config) -> bool, but VOICE, which owns #25072, depends on AI-1B. How does AI-1B build it without inverting that edge?

**Default:** AI-1B's #25105 declares the speech-to-text probe as a trait in crates/gai with #25072's contract and builds the full snapshot, the audio bindings and GET /api/llm/status against a stub probe, as the AI plan's own 4.2 verification does. The ai family takes the probe as a constructor argument and, without one, leaves GET /api/llm/status to Python, as 6.0's health seam leaves the providers rows to Python without a source. CP-WAVE1-CUT therefore activates the ai family's other seven rows: #25107's in-process tests pass a stub probe, its 5.2.2 end-to-end run on the gdaemon binary covers those seven rows, and VOICE's harness adds GET /api/llm/status with the real probe. VOICE's #25072 implements the trait on D9's engine through an adapter in gdaemon, so gai takes no dependency on VOICE's code. VOICE also composes the adapter into the ai family in crates/gdaemon/src/serve.rs, which passes it only while the voice family's resolved backend is Native, as a family's jobs register only while the family is active: on the live daemon that backend is the compiled default, Proxy until CP-WAVE3-CUT, and in the harness front_door.routes in a temporary home (D37). CP-WAVE3-CUT's activation commit then flips only the voice family's default, which moves GET /api/llm/status to Native with the engine it reports on, and restoring the pinned generation hands both back. A status reply that differs from the recorded cases is a CP-WAVE3-CUT window trigger unless the difference is a contract change that CP-WAVE3-REVIEW accepted for D9's engine. No worker op and no Python edit.

**Rationale:** The status row reports whether speech-to-text is available, which only the engine that serves it can answer: Python's probe constructs gobby.voice.stt.WhisperSTT and reads is_available (src/gobby/ai/registry.py:503-506; src/gobby/voice/stt.py:229-234), and VOICE ports that engine behind D9's choice. A worker op that asks Python's probe would add a D4 Python edit that S3-DELETE must remove. Moving #25072 into AI-1B would put D9's engine work into wave 1 before Josh decides it. Moving #25105 into VOICE would carry #25106 and #25107, which depend on it, into wave 3, although CP-WAVE1-CUT activates the ai family. Writing the composition into CP-WAVE3-CUT's activation commit instead would add wiring to a commit that flips only code defaults (D37), and an ungated composition would serve GET /api/llm/status natively from VOICE's landing, because the ai family is Native from CP-WAVE1-CUT. D107 replaces the engine, so #25072's criterion 3 (parity with faster-whisper) is an accepted contract change under D17 at CP-WAVE3-REVIEW.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D51: #25109 (AI plan 6.1) reads 2.5's get_observation context evidence, which AI-1B builds (#25100) and 6.1.3 tests, yet AI-1A owns #21587 P6 (#25090) and AI-1B depends on AI-1A. Likewise, AI-1B's #25106 (5.1.1) and #25107 (5.2.1-5.2.2) cover all eight ai rows, POST /api/embeddings among them, yet AI-2, which AI-1B does not reach, lists that row's builder (1.2, #25095) and owns the ai family's three embeddings rows. Where do the providers routes and the embeddings rows go?

**Default:** A new family chunk, AI-1C, lands right after AI-1B and owns #25090's three dormant leaves: the web-chat provider health seam (#25108), the provider list and model catalog (#25109) and provider usage (#25110), with the GET /api/providers ledger rows. AI-1A keeps all of P1, including the providers family's empty shell and 1.2's native embedding generation (#25095), and drops to one checkpoint. AI-1A also owns the ai family's three embeddings rows, POST /api/embeddings (1.2) and GET /api/embeddings/status and GET /api/embeddings/doctor (1.1), so every row that 5.1.1 and 5.2.1-5.2.2 cover is built in AI-1A or AI-1B; AI-2 keeps the four switch rows. CHAT-2 depends on AI-1C, because #25111's flip needs 6.2's usage rows and connects the native web-chat runtime to #25108's seam. CP-WAVE1-REVIEW waits for AI-1C, which lands dormant in wave 1, and the providers family still activates at CP-WAVE3-CUT.

**Rationale:** In the AI plan, 6.1 depends on 2.5 and 6.0, and 6.2 depends on 6.1, so P6 cannot land before P2. Moving P6 into AI-1B would give a three-checkpoint L chunk a fourth and a fifth checkpoint and 7K more Python in scope. Moving only #25109 and #25110 into AI-1B would still add checkpoints there and split P6 across two chunks. Moving P6 into CHAT-2 would load wave 3's single-owner chat switch with three leaves that need nothing from chat. AI-1C is the smallest chunk that keeps P6 together after P2. 1.2 depends only on 1.1, and AI-1B's 2.1 depends on 1.2, so 1.2 belongs with P1; an AI-1B edge on AI-2 instead would hold AI-1B, AI-1C, VOICE and CHAT-2 behind AI-2's switch and lease, and AI-2 sits after AI-1B in map order.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D52: #25116's build and flip rebuild collections through #25070's memories seam (MEM-2) and #25071's tool_embeddings seam (MCP-FLIP), its runner holds the CollectionRebuildSeam trait that MEM-2's #25547 adds, and #25118 activates the switch, yet AI-2 lands in wave 1 and CP-WAVE1-CUT flips the wave 1 families. Where do the trait, the seam composition and the activation go?

**Default:** AI-2 adds gcore's pub trait CollectionRebuildSeam: Send + Sync in crates/gcore/src/embedding_generation/seam.rs, declared from #25542's crates/gcore/src/embedding_generation.rs, with rebuild(physical_name, &EmbeddingConfig), project_change(source_id, physical_name, &EmbeddingConfig), and promote(&EmbeddingConfig) and discard(), whose default bodies do nothing. #25116's runner holds one Arc<dyn CollectionRebuildSeam> per kind, calls promote at flip, after the alias switch and before the switch completes, and discard in abort cleanup, and is proven against stub seams, so #25116's edges on #25070 and #25071 are dropped. Until MCP-FLIP composes the real seams, serve.rs passes none and the switch family claims no row, as the providers family claims none without a health source (AI plan 6.0). MEM-2's #25547 implements MemoriesRebuildSeam on that trait, promote and discard included, and adds no trait. MCP-FLIP's #25071 implements the tool_embeddings seam with the default promote and discard; MCP-FLIP then composes both seams from the hub pool in crates/gdaemon/src/serve.rs's pool-gated block and proves #25118's dormant criteria 7.6.1-7.6.3 and 7.6.6 in the isolated harness, driving the four switch rows over HTTP, because V4's gclient verbs replace the gdaemon embeddings command tree (D16). The ai-embedding-switch family stays Proxy at CP-WAVE1-CUT and CP-WAVE3-CUT. CP-WAVE5-CUT's activation flips it and is #25118's release; the activation restart and a rollback restart each start only when GET /api/embeddings/switch/status reports no running task (AI plan 7.6). The switch writes Qdrant collections and aliases and, through the memories seam, FalkorDB entity vectors, which hub-backup snapshots but does not restore, so CP-WAVE5-CUT's rehearsal runs its switch on scratch containers restored by hand from the cut's backup, as CP-WAVE3-CUT's rehearsal does (ruling 17). No worker op and no Python edit.

**Rationale:** The trait must exist before the runner that holds it, and the memory plan places it in gcore so that gai never depends on gmemory (memory-family 3.1); AI-2 already owns gcore's embedding-generation module through #25542. The AI plan's Decision 3 contract stands, gcore's trait in that file, extended by memory-family 3.1's promote and discard; only the leaf that adds the trait moves, from #25547 to AI-2. One Qdrant alias request flips both kinds' collections (AI plan 7.4), so the switch cannot go Native at CP-WAVE3-CUT with only the memories seam, and MCP-FLIP builds the last seam, which makes CP-WAVE5-CUT the first cut that can activate it. Worker ops that reach Python's rebuild, project_change, promote and discard from a native runner would each add a D4 Python edit and an S3-DELETE removal. Until CP-WAVE5-CUT, Python's coordinator runs any switch, and from CP-WAVE3-CUT native memory writers append change rows and dual-write while it is armed, as #25547's 3.1.3 requires.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D53: #21571's plan item 1.4, gclient config get|set (.gobby/plans/client-operator-verbs.md:702-807), is a verb Josh asked for in #21571 (client-operator-verbs.md:18-21) with no Python command and so no ledger row (client-operator-verbs.md:1161), and no chunk builds it. Does it ship in 1.0, and in which chunk?

**Default:** Ship it in V1A over A1-CONFIG's GET and PATCH /api/config/values, with 1.4's compare-and-set that re-reads and retries once on a 409 revision_conflict (client-operator-verbs.md:136-143). It adds no ledger row, selector or parity-waiver entry, so its native tests (crates/gclient/tests/config_verb.rs) are its readiness evidence. Alternative: defer it past 1.0 under D6; nothing else depends on it.

**Rationale:** Josh asked for the verb in #21571, and A1-CONFIG already serves its two routes for the MCP config tools, so it costs one verb module and its tests. It is still new capability, which D6's port-as-is rule defers by default, so Josh confirms the exception. The repository's native-extension record (docs/contracts/parity-ledger.md:29-30) is not used: an entry needs its surface in scripts/generate_parity_ledger.py's ADDITIVE_SURFACES, a Python edit outside D4, because tests/test_parity_ledger.py and the HTTP corpus replay reject an undeclared entry until S3-DELETE (scripts/parity_waivers.py:100-104).

**Resolved by:** Josh, 2026-11-04 ~14:07 CT, ruling 22

### D54: #24877 (.gobby/plans/node-install-enrollment.md 4.5) adds gobby node list over GET /api/machines, with REF, HOST, OS, GDAEMON, TARGET, STAGED and STATE columns, and gobby node update, and says Python had no machine list. Python's gobby nodes list (src/gobby/cli/workspaces.py:546-563) already lists the operator's nodes, and V3 ports it with the CLI gobby nodes and CLI gobby nodes list rows. Which verb lists nodes?

**Default:** One listing, V3's gobby nodes list. S4-INSTALL adds 4.5's release columns behind a --release flag that reads GET /api/machines, so the default output stays REC-CLI's golden, and ships 4.5's update verb as gobby nodes update. gobby node keeps install and enroll (S4-RELEASE), which act on this machine before any daemon URL resolves. Alternative: build 4.5 as written, beside V3's verb. Either way, gobby node also gains status, start, stop and restart in S4-INSTALL (node-mode 1.3 and 2.5), which act on this machine too.

**Rationale:** Two listings of the same machines under node and nodes would answer one question two ways. With this split gobby node acts on this machine and gobby nodes on the operator's fleet, and the flag keeps V3's frozen output unchanged.

### D55: Which workflow and tag release gobby once CP-PREDELETE-CUT's commit B has removed the Python console script? Today a v* tag runs .github/workflows/release.yml: a gate that waits for the CI workflow's run on the tag's commit (the CI workflow also runs on v* tags, .github/workflows/ci.yml:6-8), uv build, the PyPI publish, a GitHub release of dist/* and the gobby-release dispatch to GobbyAI/homebrew-tap (.github/workflows/release.yml:3-158). A gclient-v* tag runs .github/workflows/release-gclient.yml: the client's tests, the crates.io publish and a GitHub release of gclient-<target>.tar.gz marked --latest=false (.github/workflows/release-gclient.yml:8-196). No gclient-v* tag has been pushed yet.

**Default:** release-gclient.yml on its gclient-v* tag. Commit B deletes release.yml, which ends the PyPI publish; moves its CI gate (.github/workflows/release.yml:18-38) into release-gclient.yml ahead of the client's publish and release jobs, and its tap dispatch job after the GitHub release, with the version taken from the gclient-v* tag; changes the CI workflow's v* tag trigger to gclient-v*, so the gate finds a CI run of the tagged commit; archives the client binary as gobby in gobby-<target>.tar.gz; and marks the release latest. A v* tag then releases nothing. Outside this repository, GobbyAI/homebrew-tap's gobby formula switches to the client archive before the first gclient-v* tag after commit B and raises its version_scheme, because the client's 0.1.x version (crates/gclient/Cargo.toml:3) is below the last Python release (PyPI 0.4.6; v0.4.9 was tagged but never packaged). S3-DELETE replaces release-gclient.yml's three Python steps (the moved CI gate and the two gobby-terminal preflights) with shell steps. Alternative: make release.yml the gobby release on v* tags, with release-gclient.yml's build matrix, checksums and crates.io publish moved in beside its CI gate, and delete release-gclient.yml at S3-DELETE, whose Python tests pin it until then. Under the default, commit B drops release.yml's manual workflow_dispatch trigger and its gate_only input rather than moving them: every job but the CI gate already requires a push event (.github/workflows/release.yml:7-13, 43, 72, 95, 119), so a dispatch only checks a commit's CI run, and nothing in the repository dispatches it or documents it since docs/releasing.md, added with it in 27460b3f28 (#14821), was removed.

**Rationale:** #21573 names the release workflows and Homebrew in the switch's change set. Keeping the client's own tag and workflow moves two jobs, renames the archives, retargets one CI tag trigger and changes no version line in this repository, and the gobby release keeps its gate on a green CI run for the tagged commit. PyPI's gobby package stays at 0.4.6, its last published release; v0.4.9 was tagged but never packaged. The alternative keeps the v* tag but makes it track gobby-client's Cargo version, which is below the v* tags already pushed, and leaves two workflows building the client until S3-DELETE. Moving the dispatch would need a push-event condition on every job of release-gclient.yml, its tag-version check included, to keep a check that nothing calls.

**Resolved by:** Josh, 2026-11-04 ~14:07 CT, ruling 22

### D71: How do native agent-run completions reach Python's in-process CompletionEventRegistry before AG-WAIT, and what wakes the native waiters that exist before then? agents-family Decision 15 bridges native completions into the registry through a new Python route, POST /_gobby/agent-run-delivery (agents-family 2.0), which D4 and D19 forbid.

**Default:** AG-1 adds the completion hand-off, deliver_completion, to gagents' machine effects (.gobby/plans/agents-family.md Decision 6 and 6.1). gdaemon's implementation keeps an in-memory set of run ids, each with the message its caller composes (src/gobby/mcp_proxy/tools/agent_cancellation.py:113, 162), and one task. The task calls one allow-listed worker op on src/gobby/agents/terminal_delivery.py::deliver_existing_terminal_run, bound to the worker's database, run manager, completion_registry and run_db. That is the call cancel_agent_run makes after its commit (src/gobby/servers/routes/agents.py:807-824). After each call the task rereads the run's completion_subscribers rows, a shared table (D19), and acts on what it finds. (a) No rows left acknowledges the run. (b) A false return, meaning the run is missing or not terminal, drops it with a warning. (c) A failed call, a lost reply, a retained row or a failed reread keeps the run, and the task calls again 5 seconds later. A lost reply is an unknown outcome that the reread reconciles, never a blind retry (R0 1.3). The task calls nothing before R3's agents recovery step has finished. A gdaemon restart drops the set, and the fresh worker's startup recovery redelivers the hub machine's runs (src/gobby/runner_lifecycle_agents.py::_cleanup_terminal_agent_completion_subscribers, R3's agents step). Every native terminal transition calls deliver_completion once, after its commit and its parent delivery, even when the parent delivery fails. Those transitions are AG-1's spawn failures; AG-2's cancel, kill, stop, end, unregister, cancel_stale_helpers, cleanup, watchdog and pane-monitor terminations; and the terminations other native families make through AG-2's termination service (HOOK-2's session end and its binding of RULE-1B's step-workflow completion, PIPE-2's agent steps, CHAT-2's continue_in_chat kill and NODEHUB's node-run routes and reconciler). AG-2 builds the native in-memory completion waiters that PIPE-2's wait steps use from CP-WAVE4-CUT. deliver_completion and PIPE-2's execution completions notify them in process. A waiter also resolves from its durable agent_runs or pipeline execution row, which it reads when it registers and every 5 seconds while it waits, because Python settles remote runs (the reconcile op) and exited panes (src/gobby/agents/terminal_cleanup.py:105) until CP-WAVE5-CUT. Durable completion_subscribers delivery for agent runs keeps one owner, Python's registry, until AG-WAIT's activation (CP-WAVE5-CUT). A pipeline execution that PIPE-2 runs from CP-WAVE4-CUT has no Python producer, so PIPE-2 delivers that execution's own subscriber rows natively. At that activation deliver_completion re-binds to the native completion service and the op is no longer called. S3-DELETE deletes the op. AG-2's parked check reads the durable rows and, through one more one-line op on CompletionEventRegistry.is_awaiting, Python's in-memory subscribers, until AG-WAIT's service answers it at that activation. A transition with a composed result, such as AG-2's end_agent_run and watchdog (src/gobby/agents/run_completion.py:172-208; src/gobby/mcp_proxy/tools/agents_termination.py:114-127; src/gobby/agents/watchdog/recovery.py:770-780) or the step-workflow completion (src/gobby/workflows/engine/enforcement_completion.py:279-285), passes it to deliver_completion, because deliver_existing_terminal_run rebuilds only status, run_id and error (src/gobby/agents/terminal_delivery.py:566-598), while the subscriber's message and metadata read signoff_message and the other fields (src/gobby/events/wake_notifications.py:70-108; src/gobby/hooks/pending_messages.py:197; src/gobby/mcp_proxy/tools/agent_messaging.py:83-87). The set keeps that result with the message, and for such a run the task calls one more one-line op on deliver_and_cleanup_terminal_run (terminal_delivery.py:418-484), bound to the worker's database, completion_registry and run_db, with the same reread. A restart drops the result with the set, and startup redelivery rebuilds the plain payload, as Python's does (src/gobby/runner_lifecycle_agents.py:87-127). Task expansion's in-memory subscribers are PLANS': two one-line ops, on its subscribe helper and on CompletionEventRegistry.notify, serve them from CP-WAVE1-CUT. One caller waits on an expansion, the bundled expand-task pipeline's wait step (src/gobby/install/shared/workflows/pipelines/expand-task.yaml:60-75). Python's executor runs it against Python's own tool and registry until CP-WAVE4-CUT, and PIPE-2's step then resolves it with no op: a waiter's durable read is a function its caller passes, and PIPE-2's also answers expansion run rows through PLANS' run outcome read, because PLANS' ops reach only Python's registry. At CP-WAVE5-CUT the composed-result op and PLANS' two ops are no longer called either, and S3-DELETE deletes them.

**Rationale:** One worker op on the existing function gives the same delivery as the planned route, with no new Python. The reread is the acknowledgement, as Decision 15 rules (agents-family.md:345-390), because the helper's boolean drops the per-subscriber result. The registry is single-owner live state. Its Python callers and producers last until CP-WAVE5-CUT: wait_for_agent, remote run outcomes through the reconcile op, and the host's exit cleanup. So it moves then (D19), after PIPE-2 is native. A worker op is a call, not a long wait, so PIPE-2's waiters need a native table, and a run that Python settles reaches that table only through its durable row (src/gobby/workflows/pipeline_executor_steps.py:144-176). The composed-result op is the same delivery one level down, with one more line and no shim; re-targeting the first op at that function would move the row read and the false return into Rust and rewrite the reread that this decision rests on. CRON's feedback review op is the one long call (src/gobby/feedback/cron.py:25-28): its wait stays on Python's registry behind that call (src/gobby/feedback/agent.py:329), and native terminal transitions wake it through deliver_completion, so it needs no native waiter. The durable read takes expansion runs with one more row kind and no Python edit, where a wake from PLANS' notify would need a second binding of its seam for one wave; the cost is the 5-second recheck.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D72: How does attention's single-owner state move while Python attention writers remain? The S2.7 plan makes Python writers clients of the native store through loopback routes (its 2.3, 4.2 and 4.3), which D4 and D19 forbid.

**Default:** The durable attention_states rows are shared rows under D19. The ordering cursor (one process epoch and seq behind one mutex, src/gobby/storage/attention.py:50-76), the in-memory metadata store (src/gobby/agents/attention_metadata.py:46-160) and event publication are single-owner live state. Python in-process writers that publish through that cursor remain until CP-WAVE5-CUT: hook ingress until CP-WAVE4-CUT (src/gobby/hooks/event_handlers/_agent.py), and four more until CP-WAVE5-CUT: the host's exit cleanup, the wake path's composer attention, the node report route and Python's handoff delivery, which stays live until then (src/gobby/agents/terminal_cleanup.py; src/gobby/terminals/composer_attention.py; src/gobby/hub/remote_pane.py:192-244; src/gobby/hooks/terminal_handoff_delivery.py; D75). So AG-3's attention family lands dormant with its own compiled Proxy default. The family is the four /api/attention/ routes, transitions, the cursor, the metadata store and publication. CP-WAVE5-CUT's activation commit flips that default, together with TERM-HOST and NODEHUB's store. Until then, native writers reach Python's AttentionStateManager.transition_async and AttentionMetadataStore.set and clear through three allow-listed worker ops that AG-2 registers as their first Rust caller. Those writers are AG-2's watchdog and pane-monitor writers from CP-WAVE2-CUT and HOOK-2's hook metadata set from CP-WAVE4-CUT. The turn lifecycle reducer is not one of them: it writes the shared rows directly and touches no cursor (D73). The Rust side stops calling the ops at CP-WAVE5-CUT, and S3-DELETE deletes them. The S2.7 plan's loopback writer routes (2.3), Python attention client (4.2), Python writer rewrites and route-override refusal (4.3) and Python deletion leaf (4.5) are not built (D4, D37). S3-DELETE deletes the Python attention surface.

**Rationale:** Two cursors would publish under two epochs. The web client refetches the roster on every epoch change and drops stale-epoch events after one resync (web/src/__tests__/attention-reconciliation.test.tsx:76, 218), so one runtime's attention updates would be lost. A native roster read under a native mutex also cannot match Python's transitions (S2.7 3.1.2). Python never calls Rust (D19), so the single owner moves after its last Python writer, and native writers call into it before then.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D73: Who ports the turn lifecycle reducer (src/gobby/sessions/turn_lifecycle.py::TurnLifecycleReducer), and how does it write while Python's reducers and Python's attention store stay live? No plan ports it: S2.7 keeps it as a Python client of the native store until #21592's deletion leaf (its Q3 follow-up and ORCH-REV-2 item G3), the sessions plan leaves it to the hooks composition (sessions-family.md:2270-2273), s2-11 5.10 ports only the handler side, and #21592 has no plan. Yet AG-2's native pass calls it from CP-WAVE2-CUT, before any of them.

**Default:** AG-3 builds the native reducer in crates/gagents as a library that its callers run in process: begin_turn, enter_wait, resolve_wait, resumed_work, end_turn, record_provider_failure and block_provider_failure, plus get, a plain read of the shared row. Each mutation keeps Python's single transaction: it locks the session's attention_states row with SELECT ... FOR UPDATE, reads the session, writes the row and writes sessions.status through SES-2's status write in the same transaction, in that lock order, and makes no worker call while it holds the lock. Under D19 these are shared-row writes: Python's reducers and the native one serialize on the row lock, and the corpus proves equal row and status deltas. Before CP-WAVE5-CUT the native reducer touches no cursor and publishes nothing, as ten of Python's eleven constructions do not, because they build a private AttentionStateManager with no publisher (src/gobby/sessions/turn_lifecycle.py:284; src/gobby/hooks/event_handlers/__init__.py:118; src/gobby/servers/pending_interactions.py:76; src/gobby/servers/websocket/server.py:186-189). The eleventh, capacity recovery (src/gobby/agents/watchdog/interactive_capacity.py:38), publishes its provider-failure block today. Until CP-WAVE5-CUT that block reaches browsers on the 5-second roster refresh (web/src/hooks/useSessionAttention.ts:27, 259-261), as a hook-path provider failure does today (src/gobby/hooks/event_handlers/_misc.py:418, 498). A reducer write always leaves turn_lifecycle in the payload, so it never creates an empty row, and Python's published transitions keep running the empty-row sweep (src/gobby/storage/attention.py:491-513). From CP-WAVE5-CUT the native reducer's transitions take the native cursor and publish after commit, and the native store runs the sweep (S2.7 2.1.3 and 2.1.4; S2.7 debt, independent cursorless managers). The callers are AG-2's pass and AG-3's native terminal turn observer from CP-WAVE2-CUT, CHAT-2's pending interactions from CP-WAVE3-CUT, and HOOK-2's turn helpers and the native message processor's interrupts from CP-WAVE4-CUT, after which Python's reducer keeps two live callers until CP-WAVE5-CUT, the pending-interaction manager's Telegram question (src/gobby/servers/pending_interactions.py:136, 289) and the node report route's attention tracker (src/gobby/agents/attention_tracker.py:214, 245, through src/gobby/hub/remote_pane.py:192-244), whose writes stay safe on the row lock (D19). S3-DELETE deletes it. The observer ports src/gobby/sessions/terminal_turn_observer.py, which no plan or tree node names: TERM-1's native WS input and relay report to one turn-observer seam whose default does nothing, because Python's observer sees neither from CP-WAVE1-CUT, and from then until CP-WAVE2-CUT a mediated AGY interrupt ends its turn only on later evidence. S2.7's reducer compare-and-set with expected_payload, its status fence, bounded retry and crash repair, its waiver attention.get-roster.session-status-repair and #21592's deletion leaf are not built. Alternative: whole-method worker ops on Python's reducer until CP-WAVE5-CUT, which keep one implementation live at the cost of seven marshalled ops, a Python hop on every hook tool event from CP-WAVE4-CUT to CP-WAVE5-CUT, and two call paths in every caller.

**Rationale:** The reducer holds no live state (src/gobby/sessions/turn_lifecycle.py:277-284), so D4's worker-op clause for single-owner services does not apply and D19's shared-table rule does. A native reducer that called Python's store while holding the row lock would deadlock against _transition_locked's own lock (src/gobby/storage/attention.py:625-629). One that read plainly and sent a compare-and-set could lose an update, because transition has no expected_payload and attention_id changes only on a new episode (:558-572, 680), and adding it is a Python edit D4 does not list. S2.7 gives up atomicity only to serve a Python client of a native store, which D4 and D19 forbid, so the native reducer keeps it.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D74: AG-2's native watchdog takes over the idle reprompt and the capacity reprompt at CP-WAVE2-CUT. Both hold Python's per-terminal composer lock across their probe, drain and write (src/gobby/agents/watchdog/recovery.py:363-399; src/gobby/agents/watchdog/interactive_capacity.py:89-111). It is the lock composer_action_lock wraps, and every Python composer writer shares it (src/gobby/terminals/composer_lock.py:10-12, 71; src/gobby/events/wake.py:860) until TERM-HOST moves it at CP-WAVE5-CUT. A lock a Python task holds cannot span separate worker ops, and run_sequence holds only the lease lock for one call (src/gobby/terminals/write_coordinator.py:265-344). How do the native reprompts keep that atomicity until then?

**Default:** One allow-listed worker op that AG-2 registers as its Rust caller, composer_locked_write(terminal_id, provider, gate, phases), with a small shim in R0's worker package bound to the worker's write coordinator; the shim is why it does not fit D4's one-line clause. The lock is an asyncio lock (src/gobby/terminals/write_coordinator.py:188), so the shim runs on the event loop that owns that coordinator, as TERM-1's host-effect ops do. Under the terminal's composer lock, the shim refuses with the composer state when gate is set and read_composer's state is not empty or held (interactive_capacity.py:92). It then runs the ordered phases. A phase has an action key and steps that are text, a key or the drain (composer_drain_keys, src/gobby/terminals/composer_ledger.py:602-612), sent with origin automatic as one write or one run_sequence (recovery.py:247-280), an optional observe_resolved_async key called first (recovery.py:389), and an optional interrupt action key for a failed drain: the shim checks liveness, writes Ctrl-C and Enter under that key and checks liveness again, as recovery.py:283-308 does, and only a failure there ends the run. Any other failed phase ends the run. The op returns the refusal or each phase's outcome. The idle reprompt runs its gate before the op, as Python's does (recovery.py:326), and passes two phases, clear and send. The capacity reprompt sets gate and passes one phase: drain, text and Enter (interactive_capacity.py:157-199). A failed call or a lost reply counts as an undelivered reprompt under the same bounds (recovery.py:338-344; interactive_capacity.py:118-120), and the next pass reads the composer again, so nothing is blindly retried. Two capacity steps that Python runs under the lock run outside it. The pass rereads the transcript just before the op (interactive_capacity.py:107-110), so an agent that recovers while the op waits for the lock can still get that one reprompt. It makes a refusal's blocked transition through AG-2's attention op after the op returns (interactive_capacity.py:93-105), so a composer that changes in that gap leaves a blocked entry until the next pass rereads the composer. AG-2 lands the op dormant, CP-WAVE2-CUT's activation commit starts calling it, CP-WAVE5-CUT's stops calling it when TERM-HOST re-binds the effect to gterminals' action hold (crates/gterminals/src/write_api.rs), and S3-DELETE deletes it with R0's worker package. Alternative with no Python edit: the reprompts use TERM-1's write op and the composer-read op with no lock, and CP-WAVE2-REVIEW's packet records the race as a known regression until CP-WAVE5-CUT: a reprompt's drain can wipe text a Python wake has just staged, so that wake waits for its next replay, and a reprompt can type into it.

**Rationale:** Python's wake holds the lock across its probe and write (src/gobby/events/wake.py:860) until the native decision replaces it at CP-WAVE5-CUT, and from CP-WAVE2-CUT the reprompts would otherwise be composer writers outside it, so the composer_lock.py rule that every writer shares the lock would stop holding. TERM-1's Rust action hold cannot help, because Python's coordinator stays the host's writer until TERM-HOST and Python never calls Rust (D19). Leaving the two steps in Python needs a loop R4 keeps off in worker mode and would split the idle decision's state from its write. The shim holds no state of its own and goes away with the worker package.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D76: s2-11 ports Python's ast-based parsing (2.6's inline-interpreter classifiers, 2.8's provider-launch guard for inline scripts and 2.9's condition language) and Jinja rendering (2.13's rule templates) with crates that Cargo.lock does not have. s2-11 names rustpython-parser, and minijinja with minijinja-contrib's pycompat unknown-method callback. May the rules crate take a Python parser and those Jinja crates, and which parser?

**Default:** Yes, with ruff_python_parser as the parser. Each is exact-pinned at its newest release that is at least two weeks old when its first consumer starts (RULE-N2 for ruff_python_parser, RULE-1B for minijinja and minijinja-contrib), only in the rules crate, after a licence and maintenance check, as D8 places hdbscan and kdtree in the memory crate. The rules crate sets its own rust-version if the pinned parser needs more than the workspace's 1.88 floor. RULE-N2 wraps ruff_python_parser in one module that exposes module and expression parsing, which the inline-interpreter classifiers, the launch guard and RULE-1H's condition parser share. RULE-N2's corpus proves the choice before anything else builds on it. RULE-1B renders with minijinja and the pycompat callback (for .get and .values), registering shlex_quote on shlex and regex_search and regex_replace on RULE-N2's pattern compiler; RULE-2's validator reuses both. If Josh declines, RULE-N2 and RULE-1H hand-write a parser for the Python expression and statement subset the corpus pins and RULE-1B hand-writes a renderer for the tags, filters and mapping methods bundled templates use (set, if, elif, else and for; join, truncate, list, shlex_quote, int and tojson; .get and .values), each about 2K more Rust, and RULE-2 refuses user-authored templates outside that subset at create and update, recorded as a contract change, as D33 refuses lookaround. A further alternative keeps s2-11's rustpython-parser and accepts that newer syntax fails to parse.

**Rationale:** Python's classifiers, condition evaluator and template engine parse with ast and Jinja, so parity on the corpus needs the same grammars. s2-11 2.6, 2.9 and 2.13 rejected hand-written parsers, because the classifiers need the full statement grammar and a second expression grammar would duplicate the first; that supersedes rust-rule-evaluation 2.4's hand-written expression parser. The maintenance check this decision requires rules out s2-11's rustpython-parser: its last release is 0.4.0 from 2024-08-06, RustPython itself moved to a fork of ruff's parser, and the 2026-09-01 fix for `except A, B:` is in no release. s2-11 also rejected treating unparsed inline Python as not read-only, so a stale grammar sends valid scripts down the path it rejected. ruff_python_parser is MIT, published about weekly by Astral, and its costs are a pre-1.0 API, which the exact pin and single wrapper contain, and an AST shaped unlike Python's ast. The map treats every new crate dependency as Josh's call (D8, D33).

**Resolved by:** none recorded

**Default:** Yes. The audit's equivalence case proves the linear form on Python's engine, the file's version is bumped and the rule's behavior does not change; the rewrite lands in RULE-1A's prepared commit in a checkpoint cut's step 6 (D33, D39). If Josh declines, he rewrites the pattern himself before CP-WAVE4-REVIEW; otherwise the row is on that packet's rejected list and D33 requires his ruling before CP-WAVE4-CUT.

**Rationale:** The native engine cannot compile the lookbehind, so without a rewrite this block rule stops blocking at CP-WAVE4-CUT. A rewrite proven by a case is the standard D33 already sets for bundled files.
