### D81: How do web chat's lifecycle passes run between CP-WAVE3-CUT, which moves web chat to CHAT-2's native runtime, and CP-WAVE4-CUT, which moves the hook pipeline and rule engine (s2-11 D1)?

**Default:** CHAT-2 defines its runtime's lifecycle port and binds an interim adapter over one allow-listed worker op in R0's op table. The port carries the events Python's web chat fires today. For sessions without native hook authority: SESSION_START, BEFORE_AGENT, BEFORE_TOOL, AFTER_TOOL, PERMISSION_REQUEST, PRE_COMPACT, STOP and the plan-resolution NOTIFICATION (src/gobby/servers/websocket/chat/_session_launch.py:33, 49-50, 106-133, 308-312; src/gobby/servers/websocket/chat/_stream_events.py:87-99, 458-464; src/gobby/servers/websocket/handlers/plan_approval.py:83-95), because an AGY session's own ghook already sends its hooks to POST /api/hooks/execute. For every session, AGY's included: SESSION_END, INTERRUPT and the ask-user and tool-approval NOTIFICATION (src/gobby/servers/websocket/chat/_session.py:771; src/gobby/servers/websocket/handlers/session_lifecycle.py:59-72; src/gobby/servers/websocket/chat/_interaction_responses.py:46-80). A shim in R0's worker package rebuilds the HookEvent from the session fields and event data the runtime sends (src/gobby/hooks/events.py:122-123) and runs the whole pass that ChatLifecycleMixin._fire_lifecycle runs today (src/gobby/servers/websocket/chat/_lifecycle.py:119-656): the rule pass through the same WorkflowHookHandler that the hook route uses, under run_db and, for BEFORE_AGENT, worker_staging_scope (:245-255), so its passes serialize per session with the hook route's (s2-11 DR6); then blocking webhooks, metadata mcp_calls with enforcement off, event handlers, the pending-message piggyback, the broadcast over Python's existing WS bridge and non-blocking webhooks (:426-435, 497-656). It returns the pass's result plus BEFORE_AGENT's staged payload and pending message ids, and a second call of the same op, when the runtime reports the prompt delivered, commits the staged payload as a hook receipt and applies its variable effects in one transaction, only for the winner of the receipt's acknowledgment, where a failed variable write rolls the acknowledgment back and raises so that the next delivery applies both again, and marks those messages delivered after that commit (src/gobby/hooks/replay_decision.py:20-102; src/gobby/hooks/receipt_effects.py:166-225; src/gobby/servers/websocket/chat/_lifecycle.py:437-481). The native runtime applies the returned result, sends the block reply and holds the prompt-delivery reservation, including its clearing on a context reset (:147-166), because the Python mixin no longer owns the session. Failures keep Python's branches: inside the pass a BEFORE_TOOL rule failure blocks for safety (:258-281), and an evaluation timeout or any later failure returns no decision unless a BEFORE_TOOL carries git operation roots, which blocks (:484-495). An op call that itself fails or times out blocks a BEFORE_TOOL and returns no decision for any other event. CHAT-2 lands the op dormant, and CP-WAVE3-CUT's activation commit binds the adapter with the runtime. HOOK-3 implements the port on HOOK-1's manager and HOOK-2's handlers over the shared driver, so one session's web chat, hook and tool-call passes join one FIFO (s2-11 7.1, D1.1, D1.2), and CP-WAVE4-CUT's activation commit re-binds it with the hook and tool-call rule passes (D27); the Rust side then no longer calls the op, and S3-DELETE deletes it. In each window one engine owns every pass of a web chat session, Python's until CP-WAVE4-CUT and the native one after, so s2-11 7.14's Python guard is not built, and from CP-WAVE4-CUT no web chat event runs a Python rule pass for any session. The op's request takes R0's domain-op bound, not the 65,536-byte construction bound, because it carries whole tool arguments and outputs.

**Rationale:** CHAT-2 activates at CP-WAVE3-CUT, but the native pipeline (HOOK-1, HOOK-2) lands after CP-WAVE3-REVIEW and serves only from CP-WAVE4-CUT, so landing all of D1 in CHAT-2 would leave native web chat with no rule pass, webhooks, mcp_calls or event handlers for a whole wave, BEFORE_TOOL blocks included, while CP-WAVE3-CUT keeps rule evaluation in Python until HOOK-3. No existing endpoint serves the pass: it is a method of the chat mixin whose sessions CHAT-2 takes over, and POST /api/hooks/execute selects a CLI adapter by source (s2-11 4.1.2). The op holds no live state, because the rule engine, webhooks and handlers are the services the hook route uses until CP-WAVE4-CUT. AGY's three web-chat-fired events keep their pass, as today, where s2-11's guard would have dropped them until D1 (s2-11:89-92), and Python-hosted web chat never runs its passes in two engines (s2-11:93-98). As with D41, the op does not fit D4's one-line worker-op clause, because its shim rebuilds the event, so D4 lists it by name. Fallback, with no Python edit: CHAT-2's runtime activates at CP-WAVE4-CUT, where HOOK-3 binds the native port, and the activations that need native chat move with it: #25111's providers flip (AI plan 6.3), VOICE's default with GET /api/llm/status (D50), and PROJ-2's attachment relocation and namespace cutover (#25050, #25051), so CP-WAVE4-CUT's window, already the hot path's, carries all of them.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D82: Who runs the after-tool rule pass for gateway-served tool calls from CP-MCP-CUT until CP-WAVE4-CUT (s2-11 D3)?

**Default:** GW-2's preflight worker op also carries the after phase, in the same op entry. The gateway calls it before either leg, as D26 has it, and once more after a dispatched call completes, with the completed result; a call the before phase denies runs no after phase. A shim in R0's worker package rebuilds the completion event from the fields the before call returned, because the event is a plain dataclass with no serializer (src/gobby/hooks/events.py:122-123), and runs apply_after_tool_workflow (src/gobby/mcp_proxy/services/result_handling.py:234-287), including the direct-completion mark that the hook pipeline's after pass dedupes against (:279-287), beside the before phase's pending-context check (:150-175). An after-phase failure records diagnostics and returns the handler's result unchanged, with no redispatch (docs/contracts/internal-mcp.md:69-71; .gobby/plans/rust-internal-mcp-servers.md:60-69). CP-WAVE4-CUT's activation commit stops both phases with HOOK-3's flip (D27), and S3-DELETE deletes the op. The op's request takes R0's domain-op bound, not the 65,536-byte construction bound, because it carries whole tool arguments and outputs. The op's shim also runs the outcome tracker (D99), and each op call carries the REST request's CLI-internal marker, which the shim sets for the rule events' metadata (src/gobby/mcp_proxy/services/result_handling.py:45, 88).

**Rationale:** The contract's single policy pass is a before pass and an after pass (docs/contracts/internal-mcp.md:69-71), and s2-11 gives the gateway every before- and after-tool pass (s2-11:264). The map named only the preflight, and the compatibility endpoint runs no pass (D26), so from CP-MCP-CUT a gateway-served call would lose Python's after pass until CP-WAVE4-CUT: the after_tool rules that read a tool's output (for example src/gobby/install/shared/workflows/rules/task-enforcement/track-task-claim.yaml) for callers whose CLI runs no after-tool hook for the call, such as a REST tool call under an agent token, which Python's route enforces (src/gobby/servers/routes/mcp/endpoints/execution.py:50-57), and the step-workflow completion mark. Progressive discovery's lease tracking is not at stake, because the gateway's meta-tools write the session variables and schema leases natively (.gobby/plans/rust-internal-mcp-servers.md:69-71). The after phase needs the event the before phase built, so it needs a shim, and D4 lists it by name, as D41 does. Fallback, with no Python edit beyond D4's list: GW-2's compatibility endpoint runs apply_after_tool_workflow after its own dispatch for Proxy tools, which amends docs/contracts/internal-mcp.md:86-88, and Native and Compare tools run no after pass until CP-WAVE4-CUT, recorded as a known regression in CP-MCP-REVIEW's packet.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D96: Does the port keep Python's GitHub-backed bin freshness updater (src/gobby/runner_maintenance/binaries.py:33, src/gobby/install/bin_freshness_updater.py:40-80), which BinFreshnessConfig enables by default? Under the Rust runner Python's loops do not start, and ruling 20 and D39 let nobody promote between checkpoints.

**Default:** Retire it. R4 registers no op and no native job, so the loop stops at CP-RUNNER-CUT's activation. gobby cutover and gobby install, whose promotion V1A ports, update the hub's binaries, and S4-INSTALL's hub-driven updates (#24877) update nodes. S3-DELETE deletes the loop and the updater, and the bin_update_state table stays with no writer until a later schema change drops it. **Rationale:** On a port install the loop promotes nothing today: update_all_managed_bins skips gcode, gdaemon and ghook (SET_MEMBERS), and gterm, gclient and gmcp have no published release (src/gobby/install/version_pins.py:15), so a cycle only records a bin_update_state row that nothing else reads. Once #24359 publishes gmcp the loop would promote it on a live hub between cuts, which ruling 20 forbids. It is a background feature that 1.0 drops, so Josh confirms it as a drop class.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D86: client-operator-verbs 1.5 and 1.7 (#21571) plan a command inventory (docs/contracts/cli-inventory.toml, checked by tests/cli/test_cli_inventory.py over gobby.cli.cli and by crates/gclient/tests/cli_inventory.rs) and a parity gate over it (crates/gclient/tests/operator_inventory_parity.rs), and node-mode 2.7 (plan #25258) adds a node field to every row (carried, a task ref, or hub). #21571's disposition says gport's ledger supersedes the 1.5 inventory, and no chunk defines a parity gate over it. What are the 1.0 inventory and gate, and do the verbs with no Python command ship in 1.0?

**Default:** gport's ledger is the inventory: every Python command is a CLI row whose selector names its verb chunk. The gate is each verb chunk's REC-CLI golden replay over its own rows (D16) plus S3-FREEZE's closure with zero rows proxied (D20), which S3-NAME follows before the client takes the gobby name. No chunk builds docs/contracts/cli-inventory.toml, tests/cli/test_cli_inventory.py, crates/gclient/tests/cli_inventory.rs or crates/gclient/tests/operator_inventory_parity.rs. A verb with no Python command has no row, so its native tests are its readiness evidence, as for D53: config get|set and lease status|promote|recover in V1A, gobby cron output in V5A, gobby node install and enroll in S4-RELEASE, and gobby node status, start, stop and restart and gobby nodes update in S4-INSTALL. node-mode 2.7's node field (whether a verb runs on a node, the task that brings it, or hub only) becomes a column of #25246's node parity inventory in S4-PROOF (docs/guides/hub-node-smoke.md), filled from executed evidence on a node. Those verbs ship in 1.0, as #21571's card and plan #25258 ask. Alternative for the lease verbs and cron output: defer them past 1.0 under D6, because operators keep gdaemon's lease routes (docs/guides/admin-operations.md:354-364) and CRON's raw-output route, and nothing else depends on them.

**Rationale:** The ledger already lists every Python command with its owner, and S3-FREEZE already refuses a proxied row before S3-NAME, which is what 1.7 gated ahead of the naming switch; a second inventory would restate the ledger and drift from it. The inventory's Python test walks gobby.cli.cli: it records no oracle, so D4 does not list it, and S3-DELETE removes the tree it walks before Stage 4 adds node verbs. #25246 already expands W01-W32 into the exact verbs available at the candidate SHA with their evidence, so the node field is one column of it. Rejected: a node column on gport's ledger, which would change P0-RECORD's ledger format for rows that only Stage 4 can fill. The lease verbs and cron output are still new capability, which D6's port-as-is rule defers by default: #23458 retired Python's gobby lease on 2026-10-11, and the cron verb is the Orchestrator's 2026-10-25 amendment on #21571. So Josh confirms them, as ruling 22 did for D53's config verb.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D87: #24173's criteria 4-7, folded from #22340, ask for agent conversation channels on the communications core: global, project, per-spawn-tree private, direct messages and threads, with posting rules and wake defaults; delivery by hook piggyback, mention routing and a blocking wait for message with per-member read cursors; and a Plan Writer and Plan Adversary consensus protocol over a channel. Criterion 7 and the card's fold note leave one choice open: does an agent channel need a Gobby-native backend beside the external adapters, or do the external services alone satisfy the intent? Rulings 1 and 5 make the chunk brief the spec, so no plan document answers it and the map does. Does 1.0 build that backend?

**Default:** No. 1.0 builds no Gobby-native channel backend. Agents converse through what 1.0 ports: COMMS-1's and COMMS-2's channels over D28's adapters, AG-3's mailbox with send_message's session, parent, agent, project and global targets, HOOK-2's piggyback of pending messages (s2-11 5.9) and AG-WAIT's wait_for_coordination. These are not built: agent_channels and .gobby/plans/gobby-messaging.md's migrations 460-462, crates/gmsg, /api/messaging, read_channel, post_message, set_subscription, wait_for_message, get_consensus, per-member read cursors, mention routing and the consensus protocol. #24173's criteria 4-6 close unmet under this decision, and its criterion 8 is AG-3's (D29). Alternative: a post-1.0 task builds native channels on AG-3's mailbox; nothing in 1.0 blocks it, and COMMS-1's adapter contract does not change for it.

**Rationale:** Josh, 2026-10-27, on the fold: 'gobby-comms is required, which will give us slack access and will satisfy this'. Python serves none of these surfaces, so no ledger row selects them and no corpus records them, and port-as-is defers new capability (D6). #22340 has been parked since Josh's 2026-10-05 'Gobby messaging should be deferred', and rulings 1 and 5 retire plan files and Adversary stamps for port work, so the consensus protocol has no 1.0 consumer. The cost is that agents get no threaded private channel in 1.0 beyond what send_message and the comms channels carry today.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D60: Under the Homebrew distribution, where do gdaemon and gmcp come from once CP-PREDELETE-CUT's commit B makes GobbyAI/homebrew-tap's gobby formula install the Rust client (D55)? A Homebrew install has neither today: install provisions gdaemon unconditionally but only from a Gobby workspace build with cargo on PATH (src/gobby/cli/install_setup.py:141-146; src/gobby/cli/install_setup_gdaemon.py:134-138; src/gobby/install/bin_set_coherence.py:37-42), and under Homebrew it verifies only the gcode, ghook, gterm and gclient helper formulae and skips the managed binary installs, gmcp's included (src/gobby/install/distribution.py:18-24, 49-55; src/gobby/cli/install_setup.py:189-207, 291-331). S3-NAME drops gclient from that helper list, and S3-DELETE removes the Python install. #24359's card answers only for gmcp, with a gobby-mcp formula and a Homebrew helper-list entry, and leaves gdaemon without a source.

**Default:** One formula carries the set. The gobby formula installs the client archive (D55) plus Homebrew resources for gdaemon, gcode, ghook, gterm and gmcp, taken from the release archives of their own workflows (.github/workflows/release-gdaemon.yml, .github/workflows/release-gcode.yml, .github/workflows/release-ghook.yml, .github/workflows/release-gterminal.yml, .github/workflows/release-gmcp.yml) at the versions the client's tagged commit names in its crate manifests, with nothing rebuilt, as .gobby/plans/node-install-enrollment.md D1 assembles a node bundle. One version names one source under the release guard, so gcode, gdaemon and ghook, the stamped coherent set, stay coherent, and the tap's four helper formulae, gobby-code, gobby-hooks, gobby-local and gobby-squeeze, retire (D127); the gobby-terminal and gobby-client names in src/gobby/install/distribution.py:19-24 were never formulae in the tap. No workflow in this repository changes for this: the moved tap dispatch's tag and gobby_repo let the tap read those manifests (.github/workflows/release.yml:116-158). S3-NAME's Rust change makes V1B's Homebrew helper check verify those five binaries against the client's version pins, with gobby named as the formula to install or upgrade for each, in place of the four formula names it checks today, and under the Homebrew distribution the install verb verifies gdaemon through that check instead of provisioning it from a workspace build. Under this default #24359 adds neither a gobby-mcp formula nor a helper-list entry. Outside this repository, GobbyAI/homebrew-tap takes the formula change together with D55's switch to the client, through the pull request D127 names, and its update job waits until every member's archive at those versions exists before it changes the formula, because the client's tag and dispatch come before gterm's and gdaemon's releases in the publication order (.gobby/plans/node-install-enrollment.md E1 gate 3). Today no member has a release at its pinned version: the repository's tags stop at gcode-v1.5.0 and ghook-v0.7.2, with no gdaemon-v*, gterm-v* or gmcp-v* tag, against the pins in src/gobby/install/version_pins.py:5-15. Josh's publication at that gate is the first that has them (#24359's ruling of 2026-10-13 holds gmcp's tag for his authorization), so until then a Homebrew gobby stays unable to run a daemon, as it is today. Alternative: keep one formula per binary, add gobby-daemon and gobby-mcp formulae from the gdaemon-v* and gmcp-v* releases (.github/workflows/release-gdaemon.yml, .github/workflows/release-gmcp.yml), gobby-mcp being the formula #24359's card names, and have the check name gdaemon and gmcp beside gcode, ghook and gterm; each formula then moves on its own tag, so the check's version floors are the only guard on the coherent set.

**Rationale:** Without gdaemon a Homebrew gobby cannot start a daemon; that is already true and stays true after S3-DELETE unless the formula supplies it. gcode, gdaemon and ghook share one schema identity, which separately tagged formulae can break by upgrading one member alone, while one formula whose resources move together keeps the set coherent and changes only the helper list in this repository. S4-RELEASE's node bundle would carry the same members, but it exists only on a gdaemon-v* release from S4-RELEASE's landing on, so the formula takes the members' own archives. The tap lives outside this repository, so Josh decides its shape.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D61: S3-DELETE removes pyproject.toml and uv.lock, and with them every dependency CVE audit in the repository: ci.yml's security job runs bandit and uv audit (.github/workflows/ci.yml:467-494), .github/workflows/dependency-audit.yml runs uv audit monthly and on demand (.github/workflows/dependency-audit.yml:3-6, 18-32), and the pre-push hook and both pre-push scripts run pip-audit (.pre-commit-config.yaml:94-101; pre-push-test.sh:517-530; pre-push-test-short.sh:131-141). Nothing audits Cargo.lock today: no workflow runs cargo audit or cargo deny, and the repository has no deny.toml. Does the Rust-only CI audit the Rust dependencies?

**Default:** Yes, at both cadences. S3-DELETE rewrites dependency-audit.yml's job to install cargo-audit through taiki-e/install-action, as .github/workflows/rust-ci.yml installs cargo-nextest, and to run cargo audit over Cargo.lock on the same monthly schedule and manual trigger, and the workflow named CI runs the same step in place of the security job's uv audit. An advisory with no fixed version is ignored only through .cargo/audit.toml, with its reason beside it, as [tool.uv.audit] holds such exceptions today (.github/workflows/dependency-audit.yml:29-32). Alternative: delete dependency-audit.yml and the audit steps with the Python they audit, and leave the Rust dependencies unaudited, as they are today.

**Rationale:** After S3-DELETE the dependencies that ship are the crates in Cargo.lock. Keeping the audit costs one tool install and one command in each of two workflows; dropping it leaves 1.0 with no dependency CVE check, which today's Python audits exist to prevent. A RustSec advisory that fires on the first run is found work, as a uv audit finding is today.

### D63: Projects plan 4.6's journal turns on at CP-FINAL-CUT (D47), and plan 4.5a requires every project.json policy reader to refuse pending recovery evidence rather than read a candidate that SQL has not committed. 4.5a integrates that guard only in the Python consumers D47 does not build, and no chunk says what the native readers outside gprojects do. Which readers take it, where does it live, and what proves it before the flip?

**Default:** One guard in gcore, landed by PROJ-1 (crates/gcore/src/project_metadata.rs): it opens .gobby/.project.json.lock as 4.5a specifies, takes the exclusive flock within at most two seconds, checks .gobby/.project-update-recovery/ under the lock, and returns the committed object, absent metadata, or a distinct refusal for malformed or non-object metadata, an unreadable file, a lock deadline or pending evidence. No caller turns a refusal into missing metadata, empty rules, absent verification or a default policy (.gobby/plans/gdaemon-projects-files-docs-family.md:1010-1014). It is synchronous, and async callers run it off the executor (:1008). A caller reads in one call or keeps the returned guard held across its merge and publication, passes a held guard to nested helpers instead of locking twice (:1030-1031), and takes it before any project row lock, never while holding one (:1016-1017). PROJ-2's metadata_lock.rs, 4.6's publication service and read.rs hold the same guard and resolve a journal, and update_recovery joins R3's startup order on either default, so an entry that process loss left does not keep other readers refusing until the next projects-family read. Every native policy reader outside gprojects reads through it from its own landing, with a boundary test that seeds a pending entry: B0-WRITE (#25506's task protection policy), B0-CLOSE (task_protection_policy_unavailable), SRC (merge verification), PLANS (expansion's verification commands), AG-1 (launch's sandbox write paths and metadata copy, crates/gdaemon/src/nodes/agents/sandbox/caches.rs:204-215 and crates/gdaemon/src/nodes/agents/repair.rs:135-143), AG-4 (the worktree and clone metadata copy), CHAT-2 (chat approval and the Codex and Droid permission callbacks), RULE-1H (the stop gate's verification commands and transcript evidence), HOOK-3 (hold-open autoapproval) and V5A (hooks status, run, enable and disable; enable and disable also merge under the guard). Two classes are exempt by name: identity reads of id or project_id (crates/gcore/src/project.rs:52-77, gcode, crates/gmcp/src/daemon.rs:191; plan :986-987), and ghook's hooks_disabled read (crates/ghook/src/dispatch.rs:363-369). CP-FINAL-CUT's step 3 seat reviews a literal search of crates/ before the flip. Alternative: build the reader in gprojects, as 4.5a's targets list it, so that PROJ-2's landing edits the B0, SRC and PLANS crates and those crates and the CLI take a gprojects dependency.

**Rationale:** B0-WRITE, B0-CLOSE, SRC and PLANS land before PROJ-2, and PROJ-2 already reaches B0-WRITE and B0-CLOSE through CP-TEMPLATE-CUT, so those two cannot depend on it and SRC and PLANS would need a new edge to it, while every reader already reaches PROJ-1. Families reach each other only through public APIs and Cargo rejects a cycle (crates/AGENTS.md), gclient does not link gprojects, and the families and the CLI all link gcore, so gcore is the one home that adds no edge between families, beside crates/gcore/src/project.rs and D47's crates/gcore/src/project_fence.rs. The guard adds no trait, seam or default: it is a library call, live wherever its caller is live and removed by nothing. With no journal it only takes the flock that Python's project initialization and D64's policy writers already take, so it is harmless before CP-FINAL-CUT, and guarding only behind the journal default would strand an entry that cut's rollback left. ghook is exempt because of two facts: a journaled candidate changes only the requested metadata fields and preserves every other key (plan :1142-1145), and hooks_disabled is written only by file-only merges that take no SQL transaction and refuse pending recovery (plan :1018-1019), so no pending candidate can hold an uncommitted hooks_disabled value. Any failure there already keeps hooks on, and ghook is sandbox-tolerant and runs on every hook (crates/ghook/Cargo.toml), where it may be unable to open the sidecar and a two-second wait would stall the CLI.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D64: Python's approval and validation savers and the CLI's hook toggles rewrite project.json with unlocked, non-atomic writes today, and D47 leaves them as recorded because #25057, which would fix them, is not built. Land the fix, or keep recording the bug?

**Default:** Land it under D4's bug-fix clause (rulings 6 and 15), as its own commit with its test, in REC-PLATFORM before that chunk records the project routes, unless 0.9.0 already carries the fix; the friction queue (#25603) may land it sooner. The commit does four things and takes nothing else from #25057. (1) src/gobby/servers/tool_approvals.py's save, migrate and clear, src/gobby/config/validation_detection.py's save, which its clear calls, and src/gobby/cli/extensions.py's hooks disable and enable publish the same serialized JSON through src/gobby/utils/durable_file.py's durable_replace_text with the file's existing mode (:79-115, :175-177), whose temp file, fsync, os.replace, directory fsync and readback also close the crash case. (2) Those writers, src/gobby/project_verification/refresh.py's _write_verification (:198-220) and src/gobby/utils/project_init.py's update_project_json_fields (:606-637), which are atomic but unlocked, hold durable_file.exclusive_file_lock on project.json with a two-second deadline across read, merge and publication. (3) The savers' read-for-merge refuses malformed, non-object or unreadable JSON, keeping the file's bytes and mode, instead of substituting an empty payload (src/gobby/servers/tool_approvals.py:300-315; src/gobby/config/validation_detection.py:949-957; Projects plan 4.5a.4); _write_verification keeps its own backup-and-repair contract (.gobby/plans/gdaemon-projects-files-docs-family.md:1026-1028, 1150). (4) PUT and PATCH /api/projects/{project_id} answer those refusals with 4.6's 422 and 503 bodies (plan :1147-1149). Readers and the chat, hook and task consumers stay as they are (D47). Narrower alternative: parts (1) to (3) only, so the routes answer a refusal with their existing error, REC-PLATFORM records that, and #25058's two metadata-refusal waiver entries land in CP-WAVE1-CUT's activation commit, the flip that serves 4.6's bodies. Alternative: keep the bug as recorded until its callers go: REC-PLATFORM records it, those two entries land the same way, a malformed file keeps losing its other keys until CP-WAVE1-CUT, and the CLI's toggles keep racing until CP-PREDELETE-CUT. S3-DELETE deletes this fix with the rest of src/gobby.

**Rationale:** Confirmed from source: project_file.write_text (src/gobby/servers/tool_approvals.py:346, 370-373, 394-397; src/gobby/config/validation_detection.py:312) and open(path, 'w') (src/gobby/cli/extensions.py:463-464, 499-500) truncate in place with no lock. The two savers, reached from src/gobby/servers/routes/projects.py:510 and :514, and the CLI's toggles are the live paths; the approval migrate and clear helpers and the validation clear helper have no caller in src or tests and take the same fix, so that no truncating writer stays in those modules. A concurrent reader (ghook on every hook, gcore's read_project_id, gcode, Python's get_project_context) can see an empty or partial file, a crash leaves the file truncated, concurrent writers lose updates, and the empty-payload substitution overwrites a malformed file, for example one with merge-conflict markers in the tracked .gobby/project.json, with only the policy key, which loses the project id. This is live on 0.9.0 (ruling 15): PUT and PATCH serve on Python until CP-WAVE1-CUT and the CLI's toggles until CP-PREDELETE-CUT, so it is a bug fix and not the hardening D47 defers. D47's conclusion stands, because Python's update order and its lock-free readers remain. REC-PLATFORM is the chunk because landing the fix before recording needs no D18 re-record and native 4.6's contract then matches the recording; landing it in PROJ-2 would leave it live only from that chunk's prepared commit to CP-WAVE1-CUT's flip and force a re-record. The fix needs some refusal for malformed or unreadable metadata, and using 4.6's 422 and 503 bodies gives one contract instead of two and retires #25058's two metadata-refusal entries, so this is not an edit that only aligns Python with the native contract, which A1-SECRETS' rule excludes. A refusal raises inside apply_update's transaction (src/gobby/servers/routes/projects.py:499-516), so the row update rolls back. That save takes the flock inside the transaction, the reverse of plan 4.5a's lock order (:1016-1017), and the two-second deadline bounds the wait and answers 503; no native update runs beside it, because PUT and PATCH leave Python at CP-WAVE1-CUT's flip. A landing through the friction queue changes nothing here, because this chunk records whatever 0.9.0 then does. update_project_json_fields joins the locked set although it is atomic, because project initialization reaches it (src/gobby/utils/project_init.py:325) and a stale merge there would drop a saver's key. The other project.json writers stay as they are: _update_project_json_verification has no production caller, _write_project_json only creates the file and merges nothing, and src/gobby/utils/project_context.py's isolation copies write a new checkout that no saver can name yet.

**Resolved by:** none recorded

### D65: Two plan leaves edit settings editors under web/, and no cut lands either edit: gmcp-stdio 1.7 (#24356, Decision 12) retires mcp_client_proxy.tool_timeouts with its editor and route, and code-index 9.7 (#24713) removes code_index.nightly_repair_timeout_seconds and code_index.maintenance_log_file with their fields. D14 and D32 moved parts of both leaves without saying what becomes of these edits. What lands, and where?

**Default:** (1) tool_timeouts stays. Closed #24352's gmcp reads it (crates/gmcp/src/call_tool.rs:191-193; crates/gmcp/src/daemon.rs:160-189), so the setting, GET /api/mcp/bridge/tool-timeouts (MUX-1's row, which makes #21566's D5 registry 19 rows) and the settings editor stay, and #24356's 1.7.3, 1.7.6 and 1.7.7 are not built. (2) code_index.maintenance_log_file leaves in CP-FINAL-CUT's activation commit, the promotion at which its last reader goes; D32 keeps only nightly_repair_timeout_seconds. That commit removes its contract entry, its settings field and test fixture, its frozen config cases and docs, and any stored config_store row through a destructive migration (D22), and the cut runs gobby ui build through V1B's native verb after the promotion's restart. Its deactivation commit reverts all of it, and the cut runs gobby ui build again after promoting that build. (3) Every cutover's step 2 carries one sentence for any other web/ change it lands, a pick included, because web fixes are routine and D40's picks can carry them. Alternative for (1): retire the setting at S3-DELETE as the plan says, also deleting gmcp's fetch and MUX-1's route and adding the web edit to CP-FINAL-CUT's commit. Alternative for (2): keep the key through 1.0, which leaves a settings field with no reader (AGENTS.md rule 4).

**Rationale:** Decision 12's reason was that keeping the setting 'would mean porting that fetch, its cache, and its retry into gmcp, for a map that ships empty' (.gobby/plans/gmcp-stdio.md:168-177), and #24352 then ported it, so today's gmcp honors the setting and the plan's targets do not remove that code. Keeping it is port-as-is and the smaller change, and the retirement stays open after 1.0. For (2), the native jobs log to gdaemon's stderr and the key loses its last reader with the Python files (.gobby/plans/code-index-family.md:3787-3790, 3800-3801); D32's reason, a safety timeout, does not cover a log path. The removal sits in CP-FINAL-CUT's activation commit and not in S3-DELETE's, because that commit is already a schema step with drops, so its destructive DELETE of a stored row adds no new rollback cost, and its deactivation commit restores contract, field and frozen cases together, whereas S3-DELETE's commit stays in the deactivation build, beside a row that a backup restore brings back while no contract knows it. A stored key that the registry does not know is the state Python's reconcile_registry refuses (src/gobby/storage/config_repository.py:175-202), so the map leaves none behind. For (3), the daemon serves web/dist from disk, web/dist is untracked (web/.gitignore:2), and the landing policy says that gobby ui build is what activates a web/ edit (src/gobby/tasks/landing_policy.py:26-29, 63-73), so no landing or promotion can stand in for it.

D20 has Josh approve each drop class, and its list does not name this one. Are the two CLI rows dropped?

### D67: How does a native session status write reach Python's communications manager before COMMS-2 moves the comms connections at CP-WAVE5-CUT? Sessions 2.2 (ruling C4 (a)) plans a new Python LISTEN bridge, src/gobby/storage/sessions/status_notifications.py started from SessionLifecycleManager (.gobby/plans/sessions-family.md:983-999), a Python edit D4 does not list, and AG-3's native replay already takes the bridge's other registrant.

**Default:** One allow-listed worker op in R0's op table on CommunicationsManager.handle_session_status_transition (src/gobby/communications/manager.py:804-819), with a small shim in R0's worker package that rebuilds the SessionStatusTransition (src/gobby/sessions/status_events.py) from the op's payload; the shim is why it does not fit D4's one-line clause. SES-2 lands it dormant, and CP-WAVE2-CUT's activation commit registers a gdaemon listener on SES-2's committed native transitions that calls it. It never runs _notify_status_transition (src/gobby/storage/sessions/_bootstrap.py:55-79), so Python's wake replay sees only Python's writes and AG-3's native replay only native ones. A failed call logs, as Python's listener does (src/gobby/runner_broadcasting.py:439-447), and never rolls back the write. From CP-WAVE5-CUT's activation COMMS-2's native listener takes over and the Rust side stops calling the op, and S3-DELETE deletes it. Alternative with no Python edit: no op. The operator-target drain still runs from Python's polling and inbound paths (src/gobby/communications/polling.py:117; src/gobby/communications/inbound.py:327). What is lost is fallback_telegram_targets (src/gobby/communications/manager.py:816-819) for a native write to a non-live status: the Telegram target stays on the ended session until a restart's restore reconcile (src/gobby/communications/manager.py:176-213) or CP-WAVE5-CUT, and CP-WAVE2-REVIEW's packet records that as a known regression.

**Rationale:** Python's only status listeners are wake replay and session status communications (src/gobby/events/wake_recovery.py:47; src/gobby/runner_broadcasting.py:451). Python never calls Rust (D19) and the connections stay Python's until COMMS-2, so the call goes from Rust into Python. From CP-WAVE4-CUT every hook-driven session end is a native write, so the alternative's regression would cover all of them until CP-WAVE5-CUT. The op goes away with the rest at S3-DELETE, where a LISTEN bridge would add a Python service and a lifecycle edit.

### D68: What serves POST /api/admin/restart, POST /api/admin/shutdown and POST /api/admin/lease/handoff once gdaemon is the runner, what admits a caller there, and how is a session cookie verified before SEAM-CALLERS' verifier is live? The ledger shows the rows proxied with owner R6, and R6 and CP-RUNNER-CUT do not reach SEAM-CALLERS.

**Default:** The rows go Native in CP-RUNNER-CUT's activation commit, on the compiled default that flips the runner, with exact POST claims. After the front door's authenticate, R6 admits in a fixed order: a gobby-agent-v1. bearer is refused by presence (403 managed_activation_refused on restart and shutdown, 401 route_not_permitted on lease handoff, as Python answers a live token), then an API key or a loopback break-glass is an operator, then a browser-testing. cookie is refused, then any other session cookie is checked by sending only that cookie to Python's existing GET /api/auth/status, and anything else answers 401 missing_auth. AUTH re-binds the cookie port to SEAM-CALLERS' verifier at CP-WAVE1-CUT's activation, with the shared CORS layer, and S3-DELETE removes the adapter. Three refusals are stricter than Python and are accepted differences with native tests instead of recorded cases: a dead, forged or identity-mismatched agent bearer (Python answers its own 401 reason), an agent bearer with a valid break-glass header (Python admits it, src/gobby/servers/auth_service.py:483-484) and a dead browser-testing. cookie (Python answers 401). Restart's start proof stays Python's through one R6 worker op until CP-WAVE5-CUT's activation commit re-binds it to a Rust proof that R6 builds and V1A reuses. That proof leaves out Python's worktree, dirty-content and pin steps and verifies the SRT runner without restaging it, for the reasons R6's goal gives, and those are accepted differences too. Once CP-PREDELETE-CUT's commit A leaves no worker, restart runs the whole ordered stop and gdaemon re-executes itself, with a null backend_pid. Alternatives: the rows stay proxied until CP-MCP-CUT, the first cut that reaches SEAM-CALLERS, with a Rust watcher on the marker or a long-poll worker op; R6 takes the verifier; key-only admission; a Rust start proof from CP-RUNNER-CUT with no op. S3-DELETE deletes the restart start-proof entry with the worker package.

**Rationale:** A proxied row cannot stay under the Rust runner: Python's routes only write the marker and set flags with no reader (src/gobby/runner_lifecycle.py:315-319), and exiting the worker on them would skip R6's ordered stop. A marker watcher also fires on the marker's CLI writers, which precede a signal (src/gobby/cli/utils_shutdown.py:149; src/gobby/cli/installers/service.py:598, 646), it adds a second stop path, and Python's protected-run read goes blind at CP-WAVE1-CUT (src/gobby/scheduler/scheduler.py:120-130). R6 taking the verifier would move #24967 and its security review ahead of the first cut or write a second verifier (D3, #25068). Key-only admission breaks the web UI's restart command (web/src/lib/api.ts:93; web/src/components/app/useAppCommandPalette.ts:85). Python's own status route is the existing verifier: it answers from validate_session alone for such a cookie (src/gobby/servers/routes/auth.py:194-198; src/gobby/servers/auth_service.py:659-665), under D19 with no Python edit. The presence refusal needs no token verifier at all, so no agent token can reach a stop whatever the state of SEAM-CALLERS. A worker op on validate_session would be more mechanism than the existing endpoint. The start proof has two bindings because a restart cannot skip it, since a worker's boot refusal is sticky (crates/gdaemon/src/state.rs:245-253), and because S3-FREEZE asserts zero worker ops after CP-WAVE5-CUT, which V1A cannot feed. A Rust proof from CP-RUNNER-CUT would need the worker's source root, which gdaemon does not hold (crates/gdaemon/src/lifecycle/backend.rs:85-109), a new handshake field to get it, Rust ports of the dirty-content check and the SRT restage, and the sandbox flags before A1-CONFIG, all to replace one call to Python's own proof.

### D69: How do gclient's gobby sync and gobby install's content step reach the hub? Python writes the DB directly (src/gobby/cli/sync.py:212-216; src/gobby/cli/install_setup.py:140-165). D16 says a verb with no endpoint adds one through Found Work, gclient links no PostgreSQL driver (crates/gclient/Cargo.toml), and both verbs must work with no daemon running.

**Default:** gclient stays free of any datastore connection and execs gdaemon content-sync with inherited stdio, as V5B execs gdaemon mcp-auth, resolving gdaemon from <gobby_home>/bin and then PATH. gdaemon is the schema authority, already resolves the bootstrap's database URL and embeds the template tree, so the subcommand calls the same sync as R2's content phase. V1B owns it, it lands dormant, and it ships in CP-PREDELETE-CUT's commit A. Any other verb or install step that must work with no daemon running takes the same path, such as V1A's gdaemon handoff-guard and gdaemon start-proof. Alternative: an endpoint, with install starting a daemon first.

**Rationale:** An endpoint cannot serve gobby install, which syncs before any daemon has started, or gobby sync with the daemon stopped (src/gobby/cli/sync.py:58-90), and an HTTP hard delete for --reinstall would add an admission surface to a route family that has none. Adding a datastore driver to gclient would make the client mutate the DB. gdaemon's subcommands already write the hub (gdaemon schema apply; MUX-1's mcp-auth), so no new kind of writer appears.

### D70: The app lifespan expires every pending interaction row on each start (src/gobby/servers/_app_lifecycle.py:110-121; src/gobby/servers/pending_interactions.py:385-420), so from CP-WAVE3-CUT a plain restart's new worker would expire CHAT-2's live rows. ### D75: HOOK-2 builds the native handoff delivery services, and HOOK-3 was to start them at CP-WAVE4-CUT. Two of their steps hold Python's per-terminal composer lock across multi-step ladders with reads between writes (src/gobby/mcp_proxy/tools/sessions/_terminal_compaction.py:494; src/gobby/sessions/compact_continuation.py:599), and Python's wake and D74's op hold the same lock until TERM-HOST moves it at CP-WAVE5-CUT (src/gobby/events/wake.py:860). A lock a Python task holds cannot span separate worker ops, and D74's phases have no read between writes. How do the ladders keep that atomicity from CP-WAVE4-CUT to CP-WAVE5-CUT?

**Default:** Python's delivery stays the one live handoff delivery until CP-WAVE5-CUT, and native hook handlers reach it through one allow-listed worker op that HOOK-2 registers as its Rust caller, with a small shim in R0's worker package; the shim is why it does not fit D4's one-line clause. The op has one entry per hook site: after-tool (src/gobby/hooks/terminal_handoff_delivery.py:274, called from src/gobby/hooks/event_handlers/_tool.py:362-375), stop (terminal_handoff_delivery.py:306, from src/gobby/hooks/event_handlers/_agent.py:615), compact boundary (src/gobby/sessions/compact_continuation.py:132, from src/gobby/hooks/event_handlers/_misc.py:344), compact continuation (compact_continuation.py:422, from _misc.py:391 and src/gobby/hooks/event_handlers/_session_start/materialize.py:157) and the Codex compact watch (src/gobby/sessions/codex_compact_watch.py, from _agent.py:701-735). The shim rebuilds the HookEvent for after-tool and stop, as D81's and D82's do (src/gobby/hooks/events.py:122-169), rereads the session row for the others, and calls Python's function on a worker thread with the worker's session manager, run manager, terminal manager, runtime registry and owner loop, as Python's hook worker does. Each function claims and schedules on the owner loop and returns (src/gobby/sessions/handoff.py:667; compact_continuation.py:915-959), so the call is short and the ladder runs under the real lock on Python's side. Its request takes R0's domain-op bound. HOOK-2's handlers decide first with native ports of those sites' pure checks, so only a set_handoff completion, a Stop with a staged marker, a compact boundary or continuation or a Codex handoff PRE_COMPACT makes the hop, after the handler's own commit. A failed call or a lost reply logs a warning and never fails the hook response (_tool.py:362-375). Nothing is blindly retried: the atomic claim lets the Stop entry pick up a missed after-tool call and R3's handoffs slot pick up the rest at restart. R3's handoffs slot stays Python's entry point (src/gobby/runner_lifecycle_subsystems.py:535-561, 692) and the worker's stop keeps Python's finalizers (src/gobby/runner_lifecycle_shutdown.py:484-499) until CP-WAVE5-CUT. HOOK-2 lands the op dormant and also builds the native delivery services, over the terminals seam with the composer lock as a host effect, proven over a fixture host. CP-WAVE4-CUT's activation commit starts calling the op. CP-WAVE5-CUT's stops calling it: TERM-HOST binds the services' effects to gterminals' action hold (crates/gterminals/src/write_api.rs; crates/gterminals/src/hold.rs), renewed across each ladder's waits because a hold lapses after 10 seconds, HOOK-3's composition starts the services, their dispatch recovery takes R3's slot and their shutdown pass joins the ordered stop. Operator input typed during a ladder then queues behind the hold, where Python's lock let it through, a known difference that CP-WAVE5-REVIEW's packet carries. S3-DELETE deletes the op with R0's worker package. Alternative with no Python edit: the native services go live at CP-WAVE4-CUT over TERM-1's plain ops with no lock, and CP-WAVE4-REVIEW's packet records this known regression until CP-WAVE5-CUT. A Python wake that preflights after staging is skipped by the durable marker (src/gobby/events/live_wake.py:188-200), so only a wake already past preflight can interleave, with one write. D74's reprompts check no marker (src/gobby/agents/watchdog/recovery.py:376; src/gobby/agents/watchdog/interactive_capacity.py:91), so an idle reprompt can type into or drain a ladder while the turn settles. A garbled /compact ends in the 600-second boundary timeout and the failed-attempt path (src/gobby/mcp_proxy/tools/sessions/_terminal_handoff_delivery.py:116-187). A continuation can pass the gate beside a wake typing the same pull prompt (src/gobby/events/wake.py:612, 724), which doubles it within before-agent's three 5-second retries (src/gobby/sessions/continuation_retry.py:36-37).

**Rationale:** The composer lock is single-owner live state, so it moves after its last holder (D19), as AG-3's wake decision and D74's reprompts do; the ladders then stop racing them. Hooks drive every delivery, so D74's objection to leaving steps in Python (a loop R4 keeps off in worker mode, an idle decision split from its write) does not apply, and all delivery state stays on one side. Python already delivers the rows the native set_handoff stages from CP-WAVE3-CUT, and this keeps that one owner a wave longer. D74's op cannot take the ladders without an interpreter: each write is read-verified (src/gobby/terminals/pane_io.py:461-500, 660-824), a ladder's callbacks are Python closures (_terminal_compaction.py:515-752), and its waits run under the lock (tests/terminals/test_composer_arbitration.py:454-482). A hold op and a release op with a lease are new live state with a new crash mode, which D74 also avoids. The known regression is the smaller edit, but this is the fleet's own hot path during the port, and every handoff crosses it. The op lowers the op count, because only Python's delivery reads the runtime registry, so HOOK-2 registers no runtime-registry ops. Python's delivery keeps working behind native hooks: the interrupt observers read transcripts through their own cursors (src/gobby/mcp_proxy/tools/sessions/_terminal.py:147-175; src/gobby/sessions/transcript_cursor.py:428, 510), so R5's stop of Python's message processor at CP-WAVE4-CUT does not touch them, and before-agent confirmation polls the shared attention row (src/gobby/sessions/continuation_retry.py:43-76), which AG-3's native reducer writes (D73).

### D78: src/gobby/agents/resume_executor.py::resume_agent_run (:116-726) relaunches a parked run. The parked non-task resume retry (src/gobby/runner_lifecycle_agents.py:369) and agent-run reconciliation (src/gobby/runner_lifecycle_reconcile.py:379, 522) both call it, and no chunk names its native owner. agents-family Decision 8 gives the retry to the lifecycle monitor's jobs at CP-WAVE2-CUT, while AG-WAIT ports reconciliation at CP-WAVE5-CUT. Who owns the native relaunch, and who relaunches until it activates?

**Default:** AG-WAIT owns it. It ports resume_agent_run with src/gobby/agents/resume_executor_settlement.py, resume_finalization.py and resume_placement.py, and the relaunch half of src/gobby/storage/agent_resume.py (refuse_parked_daemon_resume, finalize_daemon_resume, register_daemon_resume_waiter, rollback_prepared_daemon_resume and increment_daemon_resume_failure_count), over AG-1's launch path and spawn placement. AG-2 keeps that file's reap half (expire_parked_daemon_session and claim_daemon_stop_orphan_reap) and the predicates both sides read (src/gobby/agents/recovery_state.py; src/gobby/storage/daemon_resume_keys.py). The native relaunch activates at CP-WAVE5-CUT with TERM-HOST and the native completion service, because it moves completion waits from the old run to its successor (src/gobby/agents/resume_finalization.py:23-36) and AG-WAIT's reconciliation, its other caller, activates there. Until then Python relaunches. R4's one-iteration op on the parked retry stays in the worker, and its entry on R4's loop list is AG-WAIT's, not AG-2's. R3's agent-run entry point keeps calling resume_agent_run. A session relaunched that way finishes its handoff at its first hook (src/gobby/hooks/agent_run_ingress.py:131-156), which from CP-WAVE4-CUT reaches HOOK-2's one-line worker op on finalize_resume_handoff_async (resume_finalization.py:58-79), bound to the worker's database and completion registry. The Rust side times that call at the 5 seconds Python's threadsafe form allows (:82-141). A failed call gives the ingress's retryable refusal, and the next hook event repeats the fenced finalization. CP-WAVE5-CUT's activation registers AG-WAIT's retry job on R4's registry in place of the op, puts the relaunch behind its recovery steps in R3's order and re-binds that finalize call to its native service. Both ops are then no longer called, and S3-DELETE deletes them.

**Rationale:** A native relaunch at CP-WAVE2-CUT would run beside Python's recovery relaunch until CP-WAVE5-CUT, two implementations over one fence, and it would need an op on the completion registry's move. AG-WAIT already ports the recovery that calls it and depends on AG-2, TERM-HOST and, through them, AG-1. R4 already drives the retry as an op, so leaving it there adds no Python. The finalize op keeps the durable transfer and the registry move in one call on the registry's owner loop, which is what the function is for; porting the transaction earlier would split the fence's owner and still need three registry calls (get_continuation_prompt, register and cleanup) or a shim that rebuilds FinalizeDaemonResumeResult.

### D83: Who ports the startup import of landed rule, agent, pipeline and variable files (src/gobby/workflows/imports.py::sync_imported_workflows, called at src/gobby/runner_init/storage.py:82), and at which cut does it go native? Every start runs it today, in dev and installed mode, over the global ~/.gobby/workflows and every registered main checkout's .gobby/workflows. Without an owner S3-DELETE would end it and 1.0 would stop syncing landed files on restart, although the landing policy classes them as restart (src/gobby/tasks/landing_policy.py:38-50).

**Default:** PIPE-1 owns the native import, and CP-WAVE4-CUT's activation commit turns it on. PIPE-1 is the last chunk whose code the import needs: the rule path runs validate_rule's dry run and the variable body is RULE-2's, the agent body is TPL's, and PipelineDefinition and the pipeline rows are PIPE-1's own (src/gobby/workflows/imports.py:89-184). Every Python import path validates (src/gobby/workflows/sync_rules.py:388; src/gobby/storage/definitions/rules.py:182-186), and it must, because a block rule whose condition errors matches (RULE-1H's DR5), so an unvalidated operator rule could block the fleet. TPL's skip of that dry run holds only for the bundled tree, which its generic test proves at every merge. The worker's startup_content phase carries five steps in one call (src/gobby/runner_init/storage.py:62-91, 299-314): the bundled sync, this import, the project skill folder sync that #25775 added (sync_project_skills, src/gobby/skills/project_sync.py), migrate_rule_delivery_dispositions and manager construction, and no separate Python sync op exists to stop calling. So R2 gives that phase's initialize body two optional members, sync_bundled and import_landed, each true when absent, which keeps a retained gdaemon's phase whole and stays inside D4's R0-R6 clause. CP-WAVE1-CUT's activation sends sync_bundled false after TPL's native sync. CP-WAVE4-CUT's sends import_landed false after PIPE-1's native import. One compiled default chooses native or Python for each step, so exactly one importer runs at any start (D37). TPL's native sync takes neither of Python's two gates on a bundled publish, dev mode and the dirty-tree refusal (src/gobby/runner_init/storage.py:47-59, 73-78): those guard a publish of the working tree, the native sync publishes gdaemon's embedded copy, and the dev-mode test needs the pyproject.toml and src/gobby/install/shared that S3-DELETE removes (src/gobby/utils/dev.py:45-83), so it runs at every hub or standalone start, where the import runs too, as Python's does in dev and installed mode. The migration needs no port: it stays in the phase until CP-PREDELETE-CUT's commit A stops starting the worker, and every native writer prepares the rows it writes. The reload_cache tool and POST /api/admin/workflows/reload move from TPL to PIPE-1, because they run the same import, so they stay proxied until CP-WAVE4-CUT and need no worker op. S3-DELETE deletes the two members with the worker package. Alternative: a validate_rule worker op, so that TPL can import at CP-WAVE1-CUT: a D4 exception for a service that is no single-owner state, with nothing gained before the rule engine flips. Alternative: RULE-2 owns the import and CP-WAVE3-CUT flips rules, agents and variables while Python keeps pipelines until CP-WAVE4-CUT: two importers at once and a per-kind orphan retirement, for one cut of difference. Alternative for the sync's gate: a native dev-mode test over the moved tree, so an installed start publishes nothing until gobby install or gobby sync runs, as Python's does.

**Rationale:** The port must keep what the Python path guarantees: a landed file becomes active at restart, a malformed file is logged and skipped, and an unavailable checkout never retires its definitions. Waiting for the last validator costs little, because Python's import works unchanged beside TPL's native sync and RULE-2's native rule CRUD: it writes the same rows and revision notifications, and CP-WAVE4-REVIEW's list of stored rows the regex crate rejects (D33) covers what it stores before the native engine reads them. The rehearsal at the flip shows any live file the native validator would refuse, while its prior row keeps serving. The import goes native at CP-WAVE4-CUT and not at CP-PREDELETE-CUT's commit A, because from that start the native engine reads these rows, and Python's import would store a pattern the regex crate rejects, which the native cache skips, so a skipped block rule fails open (D33).

### D84: Which record does a recorded difference from Python's answer take: a docs/contracts/parity-waivers.json entry or a row in D18's known-bad registry? The sessions plan (.gobby/plans/sessions-family.md:319-345), AG-3, V3 and every other family plan use entries for differences that the registry could also hold. D48's status says the citation rule for every entry comes to Josh with the map.

**Default:** One test: does the difference show in the answer of one corpus or golden case on one exact parity-ledger surface? If it does (HTTP, MCP, WS or CLI), it takes an entry and a citation with the native answer beside the recording, whether it corrects a Python accident or is an accepted contract change. The entry and citation land in the activation commit that flips that surface, with the regenerated ledger, and no chunk landing commits either; until then the owning chunk's replay reads a fixture copy (D48). If it does not, because the difference shows only in a vector below any one surface (rule-pass, normalizer, adapter, closure, capability, YAML reader and SQL guard vectors) or in a flow across surfaces (the hold-open wait), it takes a known-bad row: Python's replay asserts python_observed and native the correct value, at any commit, with no flip and no ledger note (D18). A Python bug fixed under D4 needs neither: its commit re-records the case and retires the row if it has one. So the sessions debt B3-B11 and W3 are entries, as the sessions plan has them; A1-SECRETS' and A1-CONFIG's accepted changes, MUX-2's websocket refusal and V5B's differences are entries; P0-DECISIONS' shell-guard rows, A1-CONFIG's YAML 1.1 readings, CIX-2's capability vectors, SES-1's query_readonly guard and HOOK-3's hold-open flow stay rows. An entry id follows the loader's pattern of lowercase kebab segments (scripts/parity_waivers.py:27-28), so the sessions plan's ids with an underscore, such as sessions.mcp-get_session_commits.unified-time-bounds, are respelled with hyphens. Alternative: key on whose answer is wrong, so that corrected accidents are rows and accepted changes are entries: that takes a judgment at each case, reclassifies D48's twelve Projects entries and over a hundred plan entries such as memory's internal-error-redaction, and needs a new known-bad path in both HTTP replayers (tests/contracts/http_corpus.py:63-74; crates/gdaemon/tests/http_contracts.rs:411-510).

**Rationale:** The waiver machinery already does what a difference on a ledger surface needs: one case keeps its recording beside its native answer, the ledger row carries the note, and the deactivation commit's revert restores both (tests/contracts/http_corpus.py:333-369). A known-bad row has no ledger note and no flip, which is right for a vector both engines replay at any commit and wrong for a row whose consumers see a changed answer. D53's objection to an entry applies only to a native-extension entry; a behaviour entry needs only an exact inventory surface (scripts/parity_waivers.py:82-120). The sessions plan's debt-first rule also asks for an exact-surface waiver (.gobby/plans/sessions-family.md:319-322).

### D85: What is the status on 0.9.0, until S3-DELETE, of the workflow named Release Branch CI (.github/workflows/postgres-pgsearch-smoke.yml), which runs the full pytest suite on every push to 0.9.0 (:3-10, :154-155) while D34 makes only the workflow named CI and .github/workflows/rust-ci.yml required? And what does a landing session's CI check mean when a later push cancels the run?

**Default:** Advisory: it stays on, no landing waits for it and no cut waits for a run in progress, because a full pytest run takes over 30 minutes and its concurrency group cancels the run in progress at the next push (:15-17), so at landing cadence most runs never finish. It stays on rather than off, because on a landing that touches only crates/, Cargo files or docs the workflow named CI skips every Python job (.github/workflows/ci.yml:72-78), so it is the only remote run of the Python suite on those commits, and S3-DELETE's oracle needs it on the post-deletion tree. It gets a reader: every review packet names its newest run on 0.9.0 that ran to a result, that run's commit and its result, a red run is found work fixed before the cut (AGENTS.md rule 4), and a review with no such run since the previous one starts one through workflow_dispatch and names its result, or names it superseded if a later push cancels it. S3-DELETE removes its Python steps (D34), and it stays advisory after that. The same concurrency rule holds for the workflow named CI (.github/workflows/ci.yml:16-18): a run that a later push cancelled is neither green nor red, so a landing session records that and goes on, because the later push's run covers its commit. A cut's landing freeze stops pushes, so the run of the commit a cut reads completes. The workflow in .github/workflows/rust-ci.yml cancels nothing. Alternative: required, which adds over 30 minutes to every landing for a result that a later push usually cancels. Alternative: off, through a branch exclusion in the start gate, which loses that coverage and must be undone for S3-DELETE.

**Rationale:** D34's rationale names this workflow and gives it no status, and a required run that a later push cancels cannot be satisfied. A status nobody reads would leave a red run unseen until a cut, so the packet line gives it a reader at the moment a red run still has time to be fixed.

### D99: What completes a gateway-served tool call from CP-MCP-CUT on? Python's call path wraps its dispatch in output repair, the after pass, result offload, the schema-shown record and the open-tool-error tracker (src/gobby/mcp_proxy/services/tool_execution.py:223-244, 609-634), and the compatibility endpoint's dispatch runs none of them (:677-735; D26). D82 names only the after pass.

**Default:** The gateway completes every call it dispatches: dispatch, output repair, the after phase (D82) with the tracker in the same op, result offload and the schema-shown record. Python runs the tracker last; the earlier read changes nothing, because offload leaves a failed result untouched (src/gobby/mcp_proxy/services/result_offload.py:117-118) and an envelope classifies as the result it replaced. Repair: for a Proxy tool, and for Compare's Python leg, the compatibility endpoint calls the existing maybe_repair_output on _execute_tool's result; a Native tool has nothing to repair; D101 settles who repairs after CP-WAVE5-CUT. The endpoint marks a CallToolResult, because three readers branch on that type. Offload: GW-2 ports the write path natively (src/gobby/mcp_proxy/services/result_offload.py; ToolResultStore.save) and runs it on every backend, on /mcp and the REST call routes; an in-process caller never offloads, as in Python, so HOOK-3's dispatch sends offload false; the rows stay shared under D19, Python's gobby-results pages them until MCP-FLIP, and Python's op cleans them until CP-WAVE5-CUT. Schema-shown record: GW-1 appends to unlocked_tools natively. Tracker: D82's shim calls the existing track_proxy_outcome in the op, only where the pass runs, until HOOK-3's flip, and HOOK-3's native binding then calls HOOK-2's port, under one compiled default. Two pure ports move up because their first caller moved up: GW-1 ports src/gobby/hooks/tool_outcomes.py to crates/gcore/src/tool_outcomes.rs, which RULE-N2 and HOOK-2 reuse, and GW-2 ports src/gobby/hooks/tool_error_tracker.py:74-160 and 240-305 to crates/gcore/src/tool_error_text.rs, which RULE-1H, SES-3B and HOOK-2 reuse. Fallback, with no Python edit beyond D4's list: GW-2 ports track_proxy_outcome and its two session-variable writes natively from CP-MCP-CUT (about 0.4K more Rust, and native writes of a variable that Python's hook path still writes, which D27 avoids), and a repair instance answers unrepaired from CP-MCP-CUT to CP-WAVE5-CUT, recorded in CP-MCP-REVIEW's packet. S3-DELETE deletes both calls with GW-2's op shim and the compatibility endpoint.

**Rationale:** Without this, from CP-MCP-CUT large results return whole into agents' contexts and gobby-results finds nothing to page; a repair instance answers unrepaired; and a proxied failure stops reaching open_tool_errors, which handoff summaries and the stop gate read (src/gobby/sessions/handoff_summary.py:82; src/gobby/workflows/safe_evaluator.py:744), because the hook path skips wrapper echoes (src/gobby/hooks/event_handlers/_tool.py:342; src/gobby/hooks/tool_error_tracker.py:234-237). Offload cannot ride D82's shim: the shim stops at CP-WAVE4-CUT and MCP-FLIP activates at CP-WAVE5-CUT, so a shim offload leaves one wave with none or keeps the op alive past HOOK-3's flip, and it needs a native port at the end anyway. The endpoint cannot do it either, because the after phase must read the whole output and Native tools never reach the endpoint. A native write path needs no new crate (the hub pool and gcore's sanitize_pg_search_query). The tracker stays in the shim until the flip because hook ingress's writer of the same variable moves there too (D27). A private classifier in the gateway would be a second authority beside RULE-N2's, so the classifier moves to gcore, which every consumer can depend on (s2-11 5.8's hooks crate is not one the rules crate or the gateway can depend on).

### D100: P0-LOWTRAFFIC's adapter verification and Discord's stated test need real accounts (rulings 12 and 13; D28). What does the chunk do for an adapter with no account?

**Default:** At map approval Josh names, for each of Telegram, Slack, Teams, email and SMS and for a Discord test guild, a test account or substitute, or none. Credentials stay on the port machine, in environment variables or gobby secrets that the chunk's report names only by key, and never enter the repository or a corpus. An adapter with no named account when P0-LOWTRAFFIC is claimed counts as failing verification under D28: it is recorded from its provider's published API contract, and Discord counts as not passing, so D28's deferral applies. CP-WAVE5-REVIEW's packet shows each such adapter as not run: no account. The session neither stalls nor asks.

**Rationale:** The rulings require verification before a recording becomes an oracle, and D28 already gives the failing branch, so a missing account needs no new branch. The chunk's risk line otherwise left a question with no default. The alternative, holding the chunk until the accounts exist, would stall VOICE and COMMS-1 and, through them, waves 3 and 5.

### D102: Who moves a public WebSocket connection off Python, and when? NativeWs::accept refuses without Python's binding and opens one private leg per connection, and Python's end of that leg admits the client, sends the welcome frame and answers WS message ping (crates/gdaemon/src/websocket/transport.rs:237-255, 452-465; src/gobby/servers/websocket/native_bridge.py:159-177, 216-236; src/gobby/servers/websocket/server.py:393-409; docs/contracts/parity-ledger.md:1091, 1128). **Default:** SEAM-WS builds the leg-free path dormant, on SEAM-CALLERS' Admitted value, with the registry's confinement gate and the connection events the families bind, and CP-WAVE5-CUT's activation commit flips its one compiled default. From then no public connection opens a leg, ping is native, and S3-FREEZE asserts both. Python's remaining broadcasts still reach those connections through its bound publish route until CP-PREDELETE-CUT's commit A. The cut's recipe returns new connections to Python's leg, and S3-DELETE removes the legged form.

**Rationale:** CP-WAVE5-CUT is the first cut at which every message type has a native owner, because MUX-1's tool_call is the last to leave Python, and it gives the path a 12-hour window with Python still present and a recipe back to the leg. Flipping at commit A was rejected: S3-FREEZE would then assert a path that had never carried live traffic, and a failure would land in the cut with the most to undo. A separate chunk was rejected: the public transports and ping are already SEAM-WS's rows, and the work needs nothing later than SEAM-CALLERS, since the events and the conversation-ids port are bound by their owners and the settings by the activation commit. The new edge from SEAM-WS to SEAM-CALLERS delays nothing that matters, because SEAM-WS's consumers TERM-1 and CHAT-2 already reach SEAM-CALLERS. With that edge the Admitted value and the confinement gate sit in SEAM-WS rather than with the first claimer, because the path's replay needs them at this chunk's landing.

### D103: TERM-1's activation (CP-WAVE1-CUT) moves terminal attachments, holders, generations and write admission to Rust's lease registry, while Python's host manager, composer ledger and write coordinator stay the single owner until CP-WAVE5-CUT. How does a native WS operator write reach the host? Python's coordinator refuses an operator origin whose attachment and generation do not match Python's own registry, which then holds no WS attachment (src/gobby/terminals/write_coordinator.py:575-580, 649-669). Any other origin changes behavior: daemon latches two rows per keystroke and records non-human text in the composer ledger (:857-867; src/gobby/terminals/composer_ledger.py:78), attention passes the attention gate (write_coordinator.py:573-574), and automatic reads the quarantine (:215-223). So a one-line registration on coordinator.write cannot serve it.

**Default:** One allow-listed worker op that TERM-1 registers as its Rust caller, terminal_operator_write(terminal_id, attachment_id, action_key, kind, payload), with a small shim in R0's worker package bound to the worker's write coordinator; the shim is why it does not fit D4's one-line clause. Rust's lease registry has already admitted the write, against its holder and generation for a web viewer, so the shim does not revalidate. Since #25653 only a web viewer takes the writer lease: Python's take_control answers not_web_viewer to any other attachment, its coordinator admits a gclient attachment's operator write with no lease (src/gobby/terminals/leases.py:485-487; src/gobby/terminals/write_coordinator.py:659-662), and Rust's registry already admits a gclient attachment's write without the holder and generation check (crates/gterminals/src/leases.rs:582-607). On the event loop that owns the coordinator, it takes lease_registry.lock(terminal_id), builds a WriteRequest with origin operator, calls _dispatch, which records the composer ledger's human provenance and keeps hub-mode's remote branch (src/gobby/terminals/write_coordinator.py:763-771, 805-806, 815; D21), and on Delivered releases the automatic-write quarantine (:609-610), which the native coordinator's own row release cannot do alone, because Python also keeps that quarantine in process (:745-751; crates/gterminals/src/write.rs:407-411). The shim binds no caller, so _dispatch's machine-owned check passes (write_coordinator.py:841-854), and TERM-1's ownership check at attach stands in for it, as Python's lease vouch does (write_coordinator.py:773-794). The native coordinator holds its per-terminal lock across the Runtime call (crates/gterminals/src/write.rs:361), so a terminal's writes reach the op one at a time and in order, though R0 runs domain ops concurrently. It cannot reuse _write_locked, which revalidates unconditionally (write_coordinator.py:575), and it latches nothing, as Python's operator write does not (:857-867). The row comes from one read per attachment_id, kept for that attachment as Python's lease record keeps it, so a keystroke does not queue for a worker thread (:213-214, 719-725). Its answers are _deliver_operator_write's outcomes and reasons (src/gobby/servers/websocket/terminal_ws_write.py:155-195), and a lost reply is indeterminate_backend, never retried (R0). This op serves only the native WS operator write: _revalidate_lease returns at once for every other origin (write_coordinator.py:649-669), so send_keys' write and the automatic writers stay one-line registrations on the coordinator under D4. TERM-1 lands it dormant, CP-WAVE1-CUT's activation commit starts calling it, Rust stops calling it at CP-WAVE5-CUT's activation, when TERM-HOST's Coordinator writes to the native host, and S3-DELETE deletes it. Alternative with no Python edit: the terminal routes stay on Proxy until CP-WAVE5-CUT, the fallback CP-WAVE1-CUT's risks already name, so TERM-1 and TERM-2 activate with TERM-HOST and neither D105's op nor TERM-2's two ops are built. The cost is that the terminal family's 41 rows get no live window until wave 5, and from CP-WAVE2-CUT every Python workspace read can sweep AG-1's unbound pane, because Python's sweep cannot see a native reservation.

**Rationale:** D19 keeps the coordinator, its per-terminal lock and the composer ledger in Python until their last in-process caller moves, so the write has to cross into Python, and the crossing must not repeat a lease check that only Rust can now answer. The shim reuses Python's dispatch, so the ledger, the quarantine and the remote branch stay as they are.

### D105: Every Python wake holds while a viewer reports the pane focused: WakeDispatcher reads focus_held from the lease registry and records the hold, and the registry's focus observer releases it once no live attachment of the terminal reports focus (src/gobby/events/wake.py:122, 232-246, 814-820; src/gobby/terminals/leases.py:440-469; src/gobby/runner_init/terminal_wiring.py:132-133). From CP-WAVE1-CUT the holder and its focus report live in Rust's registry, so Python's focus_held is always false and a wake types into a pane the operator has focused whenever its composer ledger reads empty, while a typed draft, held text or a blocked composer still withholds it (wake.py:821-835), until TERM-HOST binds the hold natively at CP-WAVE5-CUT. AG-3's live-wake ops run the same read. How does the hold keep working?

**Default:** One allow-listed worker op that TERM-1 registers as its Rust caller, terminal_focus_changed(terminal_id, focused), with a small shim in R0's worker package; the shim is why it does not fit D4's one-line clause. The shim keeps the set of focused terminal ids in an object with the focus_held method of wake's Protocol (src/gobby/events/wake.py:122) and installs it through wake_dispatcher.set_lease_registry on its first call, so nothing changes while it is dormant. True adds the id. False removes it and calls wake_dispatcher.release_focus_hold(terminal_id), as the focus observer does (src/gobby/terminals/leases.py:466-469). TERM-1's native registry keeps a focus flag on each attachment with Python's rules: any live attachment's report counts, lease holder or not, a terminal is focused while one of them reports it, and take clears the displaced holder's flag, release the holder's and finalize the attachment's (leases.py:144-145, 448-469, 495, 521, 547). The family calls the op on each change of a terminal's focused state, one call at a time per terminal, and re-sends its focused terminals on each new worker generation, from a step in R3's order, which runs at each start and respawn, because the set is process memory. The op is idempotent, so a failed call or a lost reply logs and that terminal's current value goes with its next change or the next worker generation; a wake in that gap reads the last value delivered. TERM-1 lands it dormant, CP-WAVE1-CUT's activation commit starts calling it, Rust stops calling it at CP-WAVE5-CUT's activation, when TERM-HOST binds the hold to its registry, and S3-DELETE deletes it. Alternative with no Python edit: no op. Each wave review's packet through CP-WAVE5-REVIEW then records as a known regression that a wake types into a pane the operator has focused when its composer is empty.

**Rationale:** The wake dispatcher stays Python's until TERM-HOST, because Python's completion registry, coordination delivery and replay still call it in process (AG-3), and Python never calls Rust (D19), so the focus report has to cross from Rust into Python. Without it a wake types into the pane the operator is working in whenever its composer is empty, for four waves on a live daemon; typed text stays, because the composer ledger withholds a wake from a draft (src/gobby/events/wake.py:821-835).
